## Part 10 — The Door That Fed the Fire

<!-- BEGIN 2016_CAUSAL THE_DOOR_THAT_FED_THE_FIRE_CHAPTER_13_NONCANON.md | git_blob f238e02a331467db03060f66ff711b65f5a92d04 -->

# The Door That Fed the Fire

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XIII candidate. NONCANON; independent literary, period Character Canon, Atlas, Librarian, Umpire and Founder-only canon/publication HOLD. Sole proposed limited interior viewpoint: fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve canonical owner characters. No human Jake-to-2016 scoring-slot association is provider-authenticated. All people, trades, physical geography, weather, injuries, decisions, structures and incidents in the prose are invented secondary-world proposals, not archival history.*

### I. The smoke without a bell

There was no bell when the mill began to burn. The boy came to the shelter without shoes.

Jake knew him from the wheelwright's yard: the narrow shoulders, the quick manner of speaking, the way his feet found a path before he had decided where he meant to go. This morning they were bare and grey with road dust. He stood in the entrance with one hand against the frame, drawing air through his mouth as though he had swallowed something too large.

"There's smoke under the upper roof," he said.

Mara was ladling the morning portion. She put the ladle down. Nobody asked whose roof. The boy had left the high mill in such a hurry that his socks were still caught in his waistband, where he had tucked them before helping wash the stones at the watercourse.

"How many people?" Mara asked.

"Inside? I don't know. The carts were there. The woman who keeps the grain was there."

"Is anyone trapped?"

"I didn't count."

He looked at Jake when he said it. Perhaps he remembered the cup marks at the shelter. Perhaps he had merely found the tallest person near the door. Jake did not know. What he knew was that the boy had run the long upper lane without stopping, and that the water jug in Jake's hands was still empty.

He set it down. "Which road?"

"The upper. Not the crossing. Don't go by the crossing."

Jake seized his coat and took the yard at a run. Behind him Mara called for the boy to sit and warm his feet. He heard a woman ask for rags and another say that the work animals must be led away from the mill yard before a cart could be turned. These voices did not wait for him.

On the hill, winter light lay flat against the grasses. Smoke had no such patience. It rose behind the bend in a brown column that leaned one way and then another as the air moved above the ridge. At first Jake thought there could not be much fire, because the column was thin. Then he came around the last stone outcrop and saw smoke pouring from the long joint where the mill's roof met its upper wall. It moved in pulses, as though something inside were breathing it out.

A cart stood diagonally across the yard. The draft animal had already been taken from its traces. Men were trying to pull sacks from beneath a covered platform, but the rope had caught on an iron bracket. Beyond them a woman was shouting for everybody to keep clear of the east storage wall. Her hair had fallen loose from its binding, and the end of a measuring cord hung from her belt. It was the wheelwright's daughter.

She saw Jake and pointed, not at the smoke but at a bucket line forming beside the trough.

"Water there. Don't go inside."

"What about the workers?"

"Counting at the gate. Go there first."

Jake started toward the open doorway. The daughter caught his sleeve.

"Did you hear me?"

He heard her. He could also see the lower half of the doorway, open enough to reveal a room dark with shadows. Someone had left a small bench against the wall. A coat hung above it, one sleeve moving in the draught. For an instant he imagined a person beneath the coat, unable to stand.

"There's somebody—"

"That's a coat. I checked. The upper room isn't clear. We don't know."

She released his sleeve as though she had no intention of spending another hand on him. Jake went to the gate.

The man at the gate had a lump of charcoal and a flat piece of scrap wood. He was making marks as people came out. Four marks stood in one row, three in another. Jake recognized some of the faces, not their names. There was a woman with a wrapped hand from the earlier grain inspection. There were two carters carrying nothing now, having dropped whatever they had meant to move. There was the buyer who had once considered commissioning a test from the daughter, his good cloak stained where he had knelt in mud. The marks did not account for everyone. They were only the names the man had heard.

"The loft," Jake said. "Did anyone come out of the loft?"

"One of them. A young one. Says there were two others."

"Where?"

"I don't know. Ask him."

The young worker sat against the yard wall with his hands between his knees. His face was streaked with soot; his eyes watered. Jake crouched before him. The man shook his head before Jake spoke.

"I didn't see them leave," he said. "I shut the stair door. There was smoke under it. The other two were near the rollers."

Jake looked at the narrow upper window. Its shutters were closed. He could not tell whether the two people had gone out another way or whether they were still behind the boards. For the first time he understood why the daughter had not let him rush through the doorway: he had arrived late, knew little and had brought his body as though that would make up the difference.

Then somebody inside shouted. The sound came once, from above the stair, and stopped.

### II. What an open door admits

The millwright who normally kept the upper machinery had gone for timber props. His assistants were carrying a ladder toward the outer wall. Jake joined them without being asked, lifting the heavy end where the ground dipped. One of the assistants glanced at him, then shifted his grip to make room. They crossed the yard together.

Above the ladder, heat breathed through the upper shutter's joints. A strip of paint had bubbled along the sill. The daughter ordered the ladder set against the stone pier, not the wooden boarding. Her father, the older wheelwright, was already there, his sleeve caught at the elbow with a leather tie. He looked at the ladder and told the assistants to bring it two feet farther from the eaves.

"We'll reach the opening from the side," he said. "Not beneath the burning edge."

His daughter looked up once, checked the proposed angle and nodded. Father and daughter were not reconciled by the emergency. They simply recognized a sound instruction.

The first rung sank into the soft earth when a man put weight on it. Jake fetched two flat stones and laid them beneath its feet. The man tried again. This time the ladder stood steady. Above them, the shutter struck its latch, once and then again. Someone on the other side was pushing it.

"Back," the daughter called. "Let them open it."

The shutter moved a finger's breadth. A hand appeared, too red to tell whether it was burned or merely lit by the fire inside. The worker on the ladder hooked the edge with a short iron tool and pulled. The shutter swung outward. Smoke rolled over him, and he ducked low enough to lose his place on the rung. Jake held the ladder while the others caught his belt.

A woman at the window leaned forward, coughing. She could scarcely see the rung offered to her. The worker nearest the top reached up, steadied her foot and told her to put her weight toward the wall. No one shouted that she should hurry. She came down one rung at a time, slowly enough for Jake to see the chalk still on her sleeves. When her heels touched the ground she held out one hand.

"There's another," she said.

"Where?"

"Behind the rollers. He was trying to turn the belt off the shaft. He couldn't see."

The daughter went pale at the description, though Jake could not know whether it was fear or anger. "The belt can wait," she told the woman. "He must leave it."

"Tell him."

They called through the window. There was no answer. Fire had begun to show along the lower edge of the upper wall, not as a sheet but in sharp small tongues, one appearing where the last had vanished. The old wheelwright sent an assistant for a second ladder. The daughter told the bucket line to wet the stonework below the opening, not to throw water blindly into the upper room where someone might be moving.

Jake saw the lower service door and remembered that the staircase had come from that side when he visited with the mill boy. The door was shut. He could not tell whether it had been barred against smoke or had stuck in its frame. He thought of the worker near the rollers, of how long it would take for the second ladder to reach him if he found the wrong window. There was a heavy iron ring on the service door, low enough to pull without stooping.

Jake crossed the yard. Someone called his name. He mistook it for encouragement and pulled.

The door came toward him with a groan. Cold air rushed into the passage. For one hopeful heartbeat he saw the foot of the stairs, the familiar worn edge of a tread, a square of pale wall beyond. Then a sound passed through the building like cloth being torn in the hands of a giant. The smoke that had been drifting slowly beneath the roof surged down the passage toward him, and flame followed the new current along the dry frame.

"Close it!" the daughter shouted.

Jake threw his weight against the door. A sudden burst of heat struck his cheeks. He felt the force of the air behind the fire and realized that his hands were holding open the road it wanted. He pushed, lost his footing on the first greasy stone and almost fell beneath the door. The older wheelwright caught the edge and pulled with him. Together they forced it inward. The daughter drove a thick wedge beneath the lower board. The gap narrowed, but black smoke now spilled from seams that had been clear a moment before.

"Go to the gate," she said to Jake.

"I can help."

"You just did what I told you not to do."

"There's a man inside."

"And now you've given the fire a way down."

Her words hurt because they were accurate. He stepped back. The skin over his knuckles had reddened. A blister was rising along the thumb he had already rubbed raw working the daughter's thresholds. He wanted the wound to be larger. If it had been larger he might have found a way to make his mistake appear courageous.

The daughter turned to the assistants. "Second ladder. West pier. We leave the door closed. Nobody tries the lower passage again."

The older wheelwright looked at Jake and pointed toward the clear yard. He neither rebuked nor reassured him. He had already taken up a plank to protect the ladder's base from falling embers.

Jake went where he had been told. For several steps he could hear the scrape of the wedge beneath the door. He had thought it a small decision, opening a way for someone to come out. The flames had made their own judgment of the opening. They had not needed his intention.

### III. The man at the rollers

By the gate, the rescued woman sat with her head lowered while the grain inspector brought her water in shallow sips. A runner had gone uphill for more hands. The smoke above the mill thickened, and the high window through which she had escaped vanished behind a grey fold that no longer lifted when the wind changed.

Jake took his place at the bucket line. The bucket came full from one hand and left his other almost empty; after a while his knuckles no longer felt like his own. He passed water forward while men at the wall spread it across exposed timber. He could not tell whether any of it reached what burned. Some spilled along his wrist, cold enough to make him gasp.

The second ladder reached the western pier. Two workers carried it upright, balancing it so carefully that it seemed for a moment the entire yard was moving beneath it. The daughter went to the gate and asked the rescued woman which window lay nearest the rollers. The woman pointed with a shaking finger, then changed her mind and pointed again. The daughter did not ask her to be certain. She walked the length of the wall herself, looking for the beam that supported the machinery floor.

Jake had seen that beam once when he helped carry boards there. It ran from the outer pier to a deep recess in the mill's inner stonework. Its joint was sound enough to carry the rollers but not meant to bear the weight of a man climbing through a window from outside. The daughter chose a different opening, low enough that a person could approach from the platform beneath the eaves.

"Why not the first?" the older wheelwright asked.

"Because the floor below it is failing," she said. She pointed to a dark diagonal line where smoke had started to escape. "That joint was already moving when we measured it."

He followed the line with his eyes. Then he helped set the second ladder beneath her chosen opening.

Jake stayed in the line. He wanted to leave it every time someone shouted from the wall. To keep doing the small necessary motion while other people approached the danger was harder than the reckless thing he had done at the service door. It gave him no single action to remember later.

A figure appeared at the second window. At first it looked like a rag caught on a nail. Then the rag moved and a hand fell over the sill. The daughter called upward. The man at the rollers could hear her, but his answer broke in the middle. He had left the belt after all. He stood at the window with one side of his face blackened, the other wet with tears that had cut pale lines through soot.

The daughter climbed three rungs and stopped. She did not climb higher. From where Jake stood she seemed terribly far from the ground.

"The floor is taking your weight," she called. "Move toward the outside wall. Then lean across the sill."

The man could not hear every word. The assistants repeated it, and he answered with a motion of his arm. He shifted, disappeared, reappeared. Somewhere inside a piece of machinery struck the floor with a terrible dull sound. The wall shook. A man beside Jake dropped his bucket and swore.

The worker on the window edge made a leap that was scarcely a leap at all. He put one foot onto the top rung, grabbed the daughter's offered forearm and nearly dragged her from the ladder. The older wheelwright caught the ladder, and the assistant below took the man's waist. They came down in a confused knot of limbs. No one let go until all three stood on the stones.

The man was alive. His left hand hung against his chest. The burn beneath his sleeve was already swelling. The grain inspector knelt beside him, asked whether he could feel his fingers and told the crowd to make room. The rescuer who had held the ladder sat abruptly on the ground. Jake thought he was wounded, but when asked, he shook his head and began laughing without sound. He stopped as suddenly as he had begun.

A gust crossed the yard. Flame appeared at the upper window where the man had stood. The next piece of roof came down with a heavy, awful finality. The daughter did not look at the people who cheered when the man reached safety. She stared at the fallen timbers.

"The small trial was laid out there," she said.

It was the first time Jake had heard her mention her work since the alarm. The measuring instruments, perhaps, and the marked boards from the prospective mill examination were in the room. He remembered her telling him the mill had asked for a carpenter and not for him. He remembered the sign she had made beside her name, an angled cut rather than the old shop's wheel.

"Can we get them?" he asked.

"No."

She said it so plainly that he was almost relieved. Then he saw her hands close, not into fists but around the empty air where her tool roll should have been.

One of the assistants asked whether they ought to move the last carts. The daughter looked from the window to the eastern storage wall and told them to take the animals toward the high gate. The grain that remained on the platform might be moved if it was safe to do so; the loft was lost. She would not send anyone into it for wood, tools or proof of work.

The buyer stood beside the gate, watching. He did not offer to pay for a new trial. Jake hated him for the silence for half a minute, then recognized that he had no right to make promises for the man. The buyer had brought a cloak and a morning's intention. That was all Jake knew.

### IV. An inheritance of ash

By afternoon the roof had fallen in along its upper run. The lower stones held, but the milling room was closed by the people responsible for the site, and the gatekeeper placed a guard at the entrance. Water ran down the steps carrying black dust and little half-burned splinters. The broken machinery inside had ceased making noise. That silence was harder to bear than the cries had been.

There was no music in the yard. The rescued worker sat with his burned hand wrapped in clean cloth. The two from the loft had both come out. Other names were still being checked by those who knew the shift. Jake refused to count people himself. The mill had workers whose names he had not learned, and he had learned what a count by an uninformed stranger could do.

The daughter stood at the edge of the wet stones, her father beside her. He was holding a length of timber charred at one end. It came from a frame outside the burned room and had been laid aside during the bucket work.

"I can cut the damaged part away," he said.

"For what?" she asked.

"A prop, until we know which wall will be saved."

"Not with that grain."

"There's wood left."

"Then keep it where the wood is sound."

She took the timber from him and turned it, showing a fracture running farther than the blackened end. They looked at it together. He put his hand beneath the fracture, measured its spread with his thumb and nodded. Neither apologized for the earlier years of work. There was nothing in the wood that could settle them.

Jake approached with the bundle of clean rags he had brought from the gate. The daughter accepted them but did not thank him. She set them beside the inspector's basin, where they were needed. He hesitated.

"I opened the door," he said.

"I saw."

"I thought the stairs might still be clear."

"They were not."

"How much did it cost?"

She looked at him then. "Do you want me to tell you that none of this would have happened without you? I can't know that. The roof was burning before you arrived. Do you want me to say your choice made no difference? I can't know that either. We saw the flame move when the door opened."

He stared at the wet gravel beneath his boots. The daughter let him stand there. She had work to do and no duty to make his remorse more comfortable.

"The man came out," he said.

"He did."

"What about the trial?"

"There's no trial in a room we can't enter."

"The buyer could come again."

"He could. He hasn't said he will."

Her father lifted the broken timber and carried it to the discard stack. The daughter watched him go, then picked up the rags and moved toward the injured man's bench. Jake remained where she had left him. The choice to speak had not bought him a place beside her.

A woman from the mill staff came around the wall carrying a small box. Its sides were scorched but its clasp still worked. She held it out to the daughter. Inside lay a square, a cord, a piece of chalk and a narrow rule, all damp. They had been kept in an outside cupboard, not in the loft.

The daughter touched the rule. She recognized it at once. Jake remembered how carefully she had rolled it in cloth before the high-lane appointment. A visible relief crossed her face. She did not look toward the buyer to see whether he had noticed.

"That belonged with your things," the woman said. "I found it after they stopped the bucket work."

"Thank you."

"Some of the drawings weren't there."

"I know."

The woman handed over the box and returned to the injured workers. The daughter laid the rule across her palm and studied the tiny nick near its end. That mark, unlike a drawing, could not explain what had been intended. Yet it was real and familiar beneath her fingers.

Her father came back and saw the box. He said, "We can redraw the joints."

"We can draw what we measured," she answered. "Not what we hoped."

"Will you work at the shop tonight?"

"Not tonight. I promised to help them secure the lower room."

He looked toward the mill's wounded wall. "They haven't hired you."

"No. They asked."

The father did not answer. He did not demand the box. The daughter tucked it beneath her arm and went toward the workers who were setting a barrier across the passage. Jake could have followed, but for once he understood that being present at another person's decision was not the same as being invited into it.

A cold drizzle began before dusk. People set out what they could carry beneath awnings that had not burned. The mill was no longer the sure place Jake had imagined when he first followed the daughter uphill. Its continued usefulness would depend on masonry, grain, hands, time and agreements not yet made. The huge unfinished wheel in the father's yard could not replace it by wishing. The younger woman's trial had not been proved, signed or purchased. Her tools had survived. That was the unequal sum the day had left her.

### V. The boy who could count

Jake walked back by the high lane with the mill boy. Someone had found the boy's shoes beneath a bench at the yard gate. He carried them in his hands because the wet leather had rubbed his heels raw. The two kept enough distance between them that their steps made separate noises on the stones.

"Did everyone come out?" the boy asked.

"The people I saw, yes. They're checking the others with the workers who know them."

"Is that an answer?"

"It's the answer I have."

The boy struck one shoe against the other, shaking out loose grit. His face had changed since morning. Jake had no way to tell what he would remember in a year: the smoke, the two workers coming through the window, his own bare feet, or something he had not mentioned.

They passed the lower turning. The barrier remained in place. Across the unsafe crossing, the old stranded cart could barely be seen in the failing light. Jake had once imagined this road as a chain of problems that could be solved if he went far enough along it. Today one building had burned while another remained unfinished, and the passage between them was still broken. There was no master problem holding the rest in its hand.

At the shelter Mara was washing cups beside the hearth. The boy from the washing place stood with his marked stick. He had stopped trying to put the same number of cuts on the stick as the number of cups people had promised to bring. A row of fresh notches ran along its clean edge.

"How many?" Jake asked.

"Eleven today," the boy said. "One is chipped. It still holds water."

Jake looked at the shelf where the old woman's cup had been kept. It was there, beyond the boy's reach unless he used the stool. He wondered whether the cup had entered the count. He did not ask.

Mara saw his reddened hands. She set a bowl of cool water before him and told him to wash them gently. When he had done so she placed clean cloth beside the bowl. He wrapped his thumb and sat near the wall, in a place where the roof was sound.

"The mill?" she asked.

"Part of the roof fell. The room's closed. Two people came out of the loft while I was there. They're checking who else was on duty. One has a burned hand."

"Did you open the service door?"

He looked up. "How did you know?"

"The daughter sent someone to tell the shelter the upper route was passable for people without loads. He mentioned a door."

Jake had already prepared an explanation on the walk home. He had turned it over and over until its corners felt smooth: he had been looking for the stairs; he had not known the flame could move so quickly; he had meant to give the worker another way out. None of these things was false. None made the act harmless.

"Yes," he said. "I opened it. It drew the fire down. We closed it again."

Mara tied the cloth on his thumb a little more loosely. She did not ask why he had thought himself the one to open it.

"The daughter tried to stop me," he added.

"Then you should tell her you heard her."

"I did."

"No. Tell her you heard her before you opened it."

He had not intended to say that distinction aloud to anyone. He looked toward the boy with the notched stick. The boy was listening, but his hands kept moving, lifting cups from one board to the next.

Jake would have liked to finish the story with the worker saved, the mill restored and the daughter forgiven. It would have been a story about himself. The actual events resisted him. The worker was alive, but the mill was damaged. The daughter still had a measure and a set of tools, but her drawings were gone. Nobody had agreed to a new trial. The boy at the washing place had counted eleven cups and no more.

"There's no wage for today," Jake said.

"I didn't ask you that."

"I know."

A sound came from the high road. At first he thought it was the missing bell, restored at last, but it was only wood striking wood: a cart wheel catching a stone, someone adjusting its load in the dark. He kept his seat. He could not help those beyond the wall from here, and the sound did not demand that he go.

Mara finished with the cloth and went to serve the last portions. The mill boy slept near the hearth with his shoes drying beside him. The older shelter boy set his counting stick in the same place every evening, beneath the shelf where the old woman's cup remained. It lay there like a tool instead of a verdict.

Long after the room went quiet, Jake was awake. He remembered the moment at the service door with humiliating precision: the cold air entering, the sound of the flame answering, the daughter's voice arriving too late because he had already pulled. He had wanted a way through for the man trapped above. The fire had wanted one too.

He would return to the mill if the people there asked. He would not open another door because it appeared to stand between him and an answer. The promise was too simple to heal a roof, and it gave him no claim upon the daughter. It was the only honest change he could locate in himself that night.

Outside, no bell rang. In the dark the damaged mill remained damaged, and the high road ran past it toward places Jake had never seen. The people there would wake with work to do whether or not he ever learned their names.

## Part 11 — What Was Left in the Yard — The Day Not Entered

<!-- BEGIN 2016_CAUSAL WHAT_WAS_LEFT_IN_THE_YARD_THE_DAY_NOT_ENTERED_CH14_V2_NONCANON.md | git_blob 6ab6b5cd3faeb5b926f4fba35d5e870772b2d7a9 -->

# What Was Left in the Yard — The Day Not Entered (V2)

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XIV V2 developmental revision of the preserved original. NONCANON. Sole limited interior viewpoint: fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve canonical principals. This is proposed secondary-world fiction, not an account of any real person's thoughts or the historical cause of a league result. The retained 2016 Flaim/ESPN archive does not provider-authenticate human Jake to scoring slot 1. All depicted people, geography, institutions, fire, injury, dialogue, contracts and decisions are provisional fiction. No 2016 postseason outcome is known to the characters. Period Character Canon, Atlas, independent Authors Council/Librarian/Umpire and Founder-only canon or publication approvals remain HOLD.*

### I. The place where the roof had been

Three mornings after the fire, the mill was more difficult to look at than it had been while burning.

Fire had given the eye something to follow. Now the eye went looking for the long line of the upper roof and found the sky in its place. The rafters which had survived stood at differing angles above the blackened masonry. A patch of white mortar had been washed clean by the rain. Below it hung a single length of belt, stiff and useless, its free end turning when the wind reached it. No one had tried to take it down.

Jake came along the high lane carrying two covered buckets. He had volunteered for the errand at the shelter and received no praise for it. Mara had told him to bring the buckets to the mill's gate and find out where the water was needed, without entering the damaged buildings. She had also told him that people at the mill could refuse anything he brought. He had understood the second instruction well enough to resent it, and then resented that he had resented it.

The girl who had run the washing place was at the gate. She was perhaps a year older than the boy who counted cups. He had seen her before only in passing, carrying bundles on her shoulder. Today she wore a shawl stitched so many times that the original seams could scarcely be distinguished. She was waiting beside a row of empty vessels.

"Who told you to come?" she asked.

"Mara. These are for washing."

"Set them there, then. Do you know which one is clean?"

He had drawn water from the shelter's covered jar, and the buckets had been washed before filling. He told her so. She lifted one lid, inspected the inside and then nodded toward a flat stone. He placed them where she indicated, not closer to the injured workers, not at the gate where a cart would have to steer around them. She moved the vessels herself.

The gate still carried the charcoal marks from the rescue. Someone had drawn a line through the first incomplete count and begun another beside it. Jake could no longer tell which marks represented people or hours or loads. He did not ask the girl. She had her own method and her own work.

Beyond her, the lower yard looked smaller without the carts. The animal trough had been emptied and scrubbed. A narrow bench stood under a surviving lean-to. On it sat the man who had come down from the rollers with his arm burned beneath the sleeve. The cloth around his hand was clean, but he held the arm close and awkwardly. Another worker was showing him a wooden peg no longer than a finger. The burned man said something, and the other worker turned the peg in his hands, testing its fit against a notch cut in a plank.

They were trying to make a door secure without the injured man's labor.

Jake had carried boards for the mill before. He knew a little of what the workers had done in the room now lost to the sky. He wondered whether he could offer his hands. He also remembered the wheelwright daughter's face when he opened the service door. He kept his hands at his sides.

A thin cord had been drawn across the damaged entrance. There was no grand seal or official notice. A red cloth hung from the cord so that a person turning the corner would see it before stepping onto the loose stones. An older carter told a young laborer not to lean against the wall. When the laborer asked who had given such an order, the carter answered, "The wall," and pointed to the widening crack beside the door.

There was something almost insulting in the fact that the work continued without Jake. He caught himself thinking it and turned away.

At the far end of the yard a trestle had been set upon three lengths of sound wood. The wheelwright's daughter was standing at it, her rule laid beside a strip of unburned timber. Her father stood across from her. A third man, whom Jake recognized as the possible buyer of the daughter's small trial, was watching without touching anything.

Jake had intended to keep away from her until she sent for him. Then her father lifted the strip of timber, placed one end on the trestle and said, loud enough for Jake to hear, "We could cut the old frame to this length."

His daughter answered, "The old frame has not been measured since the fire."

"We know its dimensions."

"We knew them before it was heated and soaked."

"It stood when the wall beside it fell."

"That does not make it sound."

Jake slowed, then stopped. He had reached the point in the lane where the trestle came fully into view. The father saw him first.

"You're the one who opened the door," he said.

"Yes."

"Come here, then. We need someone who remembers the frame before it burned."

The daughter's hand rested on her measuring rule. She did not invite Jake. Neither did she send him away.

He crossed the yard with the uncomfortable sense that he had been given an opportunity he had not earned.

### II. The length that could not be promised

The strip of timber had come from an outer lean-to, not the burned loft. Its long edge was straight and its end grain pale. The father placed it beside a darker piece carried from near the ruins. The dark timber was not wholly charred. When the daughter turned it, however, a fine split showed beneath a layer of dirt, running inward from an old knot.

"There," she said.

The father held it against the light. "I see it."

"You saw the other split yesterday."

"I saw what I could before they moved the pile."

"You said the piece could carry weight."

"I said it might. Don't turn every might into a lie."

Jake looked from one to the other. The argument was older than the fire. Their voices made that plain. He had been present often enough to know that the daughter wanted her work judged on its own measure; he had not been present long enough to know what had made the older man's judgments hard to trust.

The possible buyer spoke for the first time. "I have people waiting on a roof. They cannot wait for a family disagreement."

The daughter set the timber down. "Then you need people who can give you a roof."

"Is that not what I asked for?"

"You asked for a trial of a small mechanism. The mill burned before we could conduct it. You are now asking whether wood recovered from a fire can hold a roof, and we have not tested it. Those are different questions."

"Everything becomes another question with you."

The father looked away. Jake waited for him to defend her. He did not. His expression was hard to read from across the trestle, and Jake had already made enough guesses about the man.

The buyer pressed one thumb against the blackened side. The wood left a dark mark on his skin.

"Will you take responsibility for it?" he asked the father.

The father opened his mouth, closed it and examined the timber again. At last he said, "Not without cutting it back."

The daughter said, "We do not know how far."

The buyer looked toward Jake. "You saw the building before it burned?"

"I did."

"Was the frame sound?"

"I didn't examine it."

"Did it carry the weight it was meant to carry?"

"It was standing."

"That's something."

"No," Jake said, before he had weighed the word. "It's only what I saw."

The father looked up. The daughter kept her eyes on the timber.

Jake remembered the service door under his hands, how sure he had been of its purpose. He could have told the buyer that the mill's frame had looked solid to him. He could have said the workers knew their craft, that he had helped them, that the old wheelwright was a respected maker because other people had once trusted his wood. Every claim would have been easy to pronounce and almost impossible to recover after someone built upon it.

"I didn't see its joints," he said. "And when I opened the service door, the air changed and the fire moved. I can't tell you how much the wall was damaged before that or how much after. I didn't take a measure."

The buyer rubbed the black mark from his thumb onto his palm. His expression had changed, though not toward generosity.

"Then none of you has what I need."

"We have the sound timber from outside," the daughter answered. "We can mark what it can be used for. We cannot call the rest sound because you have somewhere you want to put it."

She placed the pale strip on the trestle again. Beside it lay her square, the cord and the narrow rule rescued from the outer cupboard. She had arranged them in the order she needed them, with no ceremonial attention to the fact that the roof had collapsed above most of her drawings.

The buyer leaned over the trestle. "Can you begin a different trial here?"

"Not of the same machine. Not on the old terms."

"How long would it take?"

"I do not know. First I need a place in which the trial can be carried out without asking people to enter that room."

He followed her glance toward the ruined mill and said nothing. The father put down the darker timber.

"You can use my yard," he said.

The offer was neither gentle nor triumphant. Jake remembered the immense unfinished wheel standing there, its spokes fixed in a circle that had never made a turn. The daughter had spent years under the shadow of work claimed by her father's name. Using his yard could mean safety from the damaged mill. It could also mean accepting that shadow again.

"I will see the space," she said. "I have not agreed to work there."

The father stiffened, but this time he did not answer.

The buyer looked exasperated by their care. He said he would seek another maker if the work could not be done. He did not threaten them; he simply named a choice that had always been his. When he left, he carried neither sample nor contract. His boots left wet prints across the yard stones. Jake watched him reach the lane and disappear behind the bend.

He had thought the daughter would be relieved to have refused the damaged timber. Instead she stood very still.

"Did I make it worse?" he asked.

She glanced at him. "Which part?"

"The buyer leaving."

"He was leaving before you spoke."

"Would he have stayed if I had told him the frame looked strong?"

"Perhaps. Would you have wanted him to?"

Jake could not answer without making the question about the courage he thought she ought to admire. She took the dark timber from the trestle and set it on the discard pile, separate from the pieces fit for cutting.

"Will you be able to work?" he asked.

"Not at that mill today."

"And afterward?"

"You have been asking me that since before the fire."

He understood that she had given him an answer, though it had not contained the future he wanted her to promise.

### III. A room without a roof

At noon a stretch of clear sky opened above the yard. The workers carried their benches into the light. Some brought food from parcels. Others worked through the meal, taking turns at the one sound vise bolted to a block near the remaining wall.

Jake sat at the far end of the yard, a little apart from the people eating. He had been given a bowl of broth by the girl at the gate, who told him it was for bringing water and no other reason. He took it because refusing would have made a spectacle of her courtesy.

The burned worker was trying to tie a cord around a bundle of pegs. He could hold the cord beneath his foot, but his injured hand would not close reliably. The first knot loosened and the pegs scattered. He stared at them for a moment, then began to gather them with his good hand.

Jake rose, thinking he might help. Before he could reach the bench, the other worker who had been fitting the door came over. He gathered the farthest pegs and put them in a small box, then placed the box beside the injured man.

"Do you want me to tie it?" he asked.

"No. Put the cord where I can hold it."

The other worker did as he was asked. The burned man set one end beneath his boot, looped the cord with his good hand and drew it tight by moving his foot away. His first knot was awkward. His second held.

Jake stopped beside the trestle, pretending to examine a length of oak. The burned man looked up.

"You can bring that board here," he said.

Jake lifted it. The board was longer than the bench and rough where the bark had been stripped. He carried it over without trying to use the moment as a confession.

"Set it on the two blocks."

He obeyed.

"Now hold it still while I mark."

The burned worker lifted a charcoal stub. He leaned his body over the board to steady the injured arm against his side, then marked a short line at the edge. He studied it, rubbed out half and drew it again. The other worker watched without correcting him.

Jake's hands began to ache from keeping the board still. He shifted one palm and the edge moved a fraction. The injured man noticed.

"Not there. I need it square."

"Sorry."

"Keep it square, then."

Jake reset his grip. He had imagined that helping the burned man would include a conversation about the fire. Instead the man wanted the board held. Jake felt the old impulse to force a reckoning: to say he had opened the door, to apologize again, to demand to know whether the man's arm had been burned because of him.

The man might know something Jake did not. He might not. Jake had no right to turn his need for certainty into another man's work.

The injured worker finished marking, lifted the charcoal and said, "That will do."

"Shall I carry it to the vise?"

"Ask him," the man said, nodding toward his companion. "He's cutting."

The companion looked at the marks and said the board was too long for the vise; they would use the bench saw outside. Jake carried it there and held the far end while the saw moved through the grain. Each stroke drew a faint smell of fresh wood out of the board. The smell was so clean that for an instant Jake thought of the smoke again and had to concentrate on where his hands rested.

They did not repair the mill that afternoon. They made a brace for the surviving lean-to, a place where someone could sit dry while the damaged room was examined. It was small work that neither replaced a roof nor restored the wages lost with the machinery. The brace mattered because a roof still existed above the bench and might not stay there without it.

At the end of the cutting the injured man asked for the offcuts. He chose two and pushed the others away.

"What will you do with those?" Jake asked.

"Use them."

"For what?"

"I'll know when I need them."

Jake was ashamed of the irritation he felt at not being entrusted with an answer. He had come to work in a place where people owed him no explanation. It was a fact he could understand in his head and still find difficult with his body.

The wheelwright's father appeared at the bench carrying a wrapped tool. He opened the cloth to show an old plane whose blade had been newly sharpened. He held it toward the injured worker.

"Until your grip comes back," he said. "The handle is broad."

The man tried the handle with his good hand, balanced the plane and shook his head.

"Too heavy. It will turn when I push."

"I can shave the handle."

"The weight is in the iron."

The father examined the tool, then rewrapped it. His face reddened. Jake expected him to insist. Instead he asked, "What would help?"

"A lighter block. With the iron set shallower."

"I have one at the shop."

"Then bring that one, if you are willing."

The old man nodded and went toward the gate. Jake watched him without speaking. The great unfinished wheel was still standing in his yard. It did not make him incapable of listening when the work required it.

The burned worker returned to the pegs, trying a different grip. His companion moved the box closer. Across the yard the girl at the gate called for a fresh bucket. Jake looked at the injured man to see whether he had finished with the board.

"Go," the man said. "We've got it."

Jake went.

### IV. The shape of an offer

The daughter came to the shelter before evening. Mara was cutting a narrow strip from the edge of a worn cloth, saving the larger piece for a sleeping mat. The boy with the notched stick was counting bowls by laying them in rows on the floor. He had stopped putting notches for anything not present. When a cup cracked, he turned it upside down and set it apart rather than trying to decide whether a cracked cup should count.

The daughter stood in the doorway until Mara looked up.

"Is there a place where I can set some measurements?" she asked.

Mara pointed to the empty end of a table. "If you are using it for work, yes. There is food on the other end."

"I will not get in the way."

"You may. We will tell you if you do."

Jake had come back ahead of her with empty water buckets. He watched from the far wall while she unwrapped the rule, square and cord. The darkened box was under one arm. She laid three small pieces of wood on the table and began marking them. The pieces did not match. One was a short wedge, one a slim offcut, one a thick corner cut at an angle.

The boy abandoned his bowls and came to look.

"What are you making?"

"Nothing yet."

"Why are they different?"

"Because I am trying to find out which one holds."

"Which one holds what?"

"A piece that must not fall."

He studied the wedges. "If you want them the same, I can bring you a straight one."

"I don't want them the same."

"Then why measure them?"

"Because knowing they are different is not enough. I need to know how."

She gave him the narrow rule to hold against the table edge. His fingers struggled to keep it straight. Rather than take it away she showed him where to rest his thumb. They tried twice before he found a steady grip. Jake felt something loosen at the sight: not a debt forgiven, but the possibility that instruction could pass between people without one of them surrendering the work.

Mara brought a bowl and placed it by the daughter's elbow. "Eat before it cools."

"Thank you."

"Are the mill people coming here?"

"Some may. They have not decided how many, or for how long. The lean-to is standing. There are other places on the upper road. They want to choose where they sleep."

Mara nodded. She did not promise more room than the shelter possessed.

The daughter looked toward the high shelf where the old bread-maker's cup stood. "Is that hers?"

"She left it," Mara said. "She did not leave us a charge to find her."

The daughter considered the cup, then looked back at the table. Jake wondered whether she had known the old woman. He did not ask. He had begun to learn that other people's histories did not begin when he was permitted to hear them.

Mara took her bowl to the hearth and left her to the measures.

After a time the daughter addressed Jake without turning.

"You held the board for the injured man."

"Yes."

"He asked me who you were."

"What did you tell him?"

"Your name."

Jake waited. He imagined she had told the man everything: the service door, the draught, the false confidence. He imagined she had made him into a warning. Then he recognized that he was imagining her words because he could not bear the uncertainty of them.

"Did he ask anything else?" he said.

"No."

She adjusted the rule.

"I should have listened to you," Jake said.

"Yes."

"I thought—"

"I know what you thought. You have told me."

The sentence was quiet enough not to disturb the boy with his bowls. That made it more difficult to hear than if she had shouted.

"I wanted to do something useful today," he said.

"You held a board."

"That doesn't put the mill back."

"No."

"I know."

"Then stop asking the work to make the fire unhappen."

Jake looked at her tools. He saw the nick on the narrow rule and the slight bend at the edge of the square where a blow had fallen long before he knew her. They were not relics. She was using them.

"Will you work in your father's yard?" he asked.

"I went to see it."

"And?"

"The outside space may be large enough for a small trial. The roof of his workshop is sound. I would need a bench that is mine to arrange, and he would have to agree not to change the measures after I mark them."

"Will he?"

"He has not agreed."

"Could I ask him?"

She turned to face him. "Why would you be the one to ask?"

The question contained no cruelty. It contained only the shape of the life she was trying to make: a place where she could speak for her own work, including when she was frightened the answer would be no.

Jake stared at the wood in her hand. "You should ask him."

"I have."

He was relieved to have been stopped before offering himself as negotiator, and disappointed to find that the relief gave him nothing to do.

She put down the wedge and moved the slim offcut against it. For the first time the two pieces fitted at an angle that seemed deliberate.

"Does it hold?" the boy asked.

"Under its own weight," she said.

"Is that enough?"

"No. But it is where we start."

### V. A sound that was not the wheel

Near dusk the older wheelwright arrived at the shelter with the plane the injured worker had requested. It was a lighter tool, worn at the edges and wrapped carefully against the rain. He had intended to take it uphill but found that the man was spending the night under a neighbor's roof. The wheelwright asked Mara if she could keep it dry until someone carried it the next morning.

Mara examined the wrapping and said she could. She would make no promise that the injured worker would use it. The old man accepted that without objection.

He saw his daughter at the table. For a moment they stood opposite each other with the small ordinary distance of people who knew every way to injure one another.

"I measured the yard," she said.

"I saw your chalk marks."

"They'll wash away in the rain."

"I know."

"I need a bench that won't be moved. And if I use the covered side, the work there must be kept apart from the great wheel."

"There's room."

"Room is not the same as permission."

"I said you could use it."

"You said I could use your yard. I need to know whether I can set my own work there."

The old man looked down at the table. The little wooden pieces seemed absurd beside the wheel he had built with so much ambition. Jake remembered walking past that wheel, how enormous it had seemed the first time, how much he had wanted to see it turn. It had not turned, and nothing in its stillness had answered what its maker owed his daughter.

"The bench nearest the outer door is free," the father said. "You may put your name on it."

She waited.

"And I will not move the measures," he added.

"You may look at them."

"Of course."

"Looking is not changing."

He gave a short, tired laugh. It held no amusement Jake could locate. "You have made yourself plain."

"I've tried."

"Then tomorrow."

"Tomorrow I will bring the pieces and see if the floor is level. That is not a contract with the buyer."

"I did not say it was."

They had not reconciled. The father had yielded a bench; the daughter had accepted the right to inspect it. It was both very little and more than Jake had expected. Neither asked him to witness the arrangement. He was only standing in the room where it happened.

The father glanced toward him.

"Are your hands healed?"

"Mostly."

"Good. Then don't put them under a load you haven't measured."

Jake could not tell whether this was kindness or rebuke. For once he let it be both.

The old wheelwright left the plane with Mara and went out. The daughter gathered her pieces, leaving a bit of chalk dust on the table. Mara wiped it away when the bowls were set down for the evening meal, and no one complained that craft and supper had briefly occupied the same board.

The boy with the notched stick held up the cracked cup. "Do I count it?"

"Can it hold anything?" Mara asked.

"Water leaks out."

"Then keep it for something that does not need water."

The boy turned the cup over and considered its possibilities. Jake almost told him it was worthless. He remembered the little pieces of timber, the burned man's offcuts, the plane that was too heavy and the other one that might be light enough. Worth was not a word he had earned the right to apply to every damaged thing.

Outside, the weather moved along the high road. A cart passed slowly, its wheel striking the familiar stone at the bend. The sound came through the shelter wall in two sharp taps. Jake heard it and did not rise. There was no bell and no summons, only someone making a difficult passage at the speed the road allowed.

He thought of the mill without its roof. The injured man had been given a lighter plane, though he had not yet accepted it. The daughter had a bench she might be allowed to arrange, though nothing had been tested and no buyer had returned. The old wheelwright still had his great unfinished circle. The workers had made one brace beneath the surviving lean-to, and there would be more work tomorrow than any one person could claim.

Jake had held a board. The fact seemed small enough to disappear beside the fire. It did not disappear. His hands still carried its pressure along the palms, not as punishment, not as a sign of redemption, but as the memory of a useful task requested and completed.

He had wanted, once, to make himself the answer to every unfinished thing. Tonight the unfinished things belonged to other people as much as to him. He could help when they asked; he could also bear the knowledge that help would not always be asked for.

The sound from the road faded. Inside, the boy began counting the bowls that were actually there.

Jake listened until he reached the last one.


### VI. The day not entered

In the morning a different kind of damage came to light.

The mill had not opened. Nobody had been told to expect it to open. Yet at the gate people waited in the familiar shape of a workday, with coats buttoned, tools wrapped in cloth, and bread hidden where it would remain dry until noon. The habits of labor were harder to dismiss than the men who had employed them.

Jake had come to return an empty water vessel. He saw the people at the gate before they saw him. He could have put the vessel beside the washing stones and gone on. Instead he stayed where the shadow of the broken roof crossed the road, because the mill keeper had brought out a board, and on the board was a list.

It was not the list of the dead. That was what Jake thought at first, and his stomach clenched at his own stupidity. There had been no deaths in the fire, so far as anyone at the shelter had been told. He knew the difference between a death and the loss of a day's work. The difference was real. It did not make the second loss small.

The board carried two headings in the keeper's cramped hand. Under the first were those who could be set to clearing the sound parts of the yard. The second heading was blank, although several names had been lightly scored through in the first column.

"Is the second column for the injured?" asked a woman with red hands.

"For those who cannot be assigned," the keeper said.

"Then why don't you put that on it?"

"Because we do not yet know which work can be assigned."

"That is different from not knowing whether we were here."

The keeper set the chalk on the board's narrow ledge. Behind him two men shifted a stack of planks a hand's breadth away from the ruined wall. The movement was so small Jake might have mistaken it for idleness if the wall had not shivered when the first plank scraped the ground.

The older wheelwright approached carrying the lighter plane in its cloth. He had taken it from Mara before the shelter's first meal. His daughter walked a little behind him, with her measuring rule and a small pouch of wedges. She had been granted a bench in her father's yard. Jake watched her pass the gate without pausing. The bench had not turned into a contract overnight, and no buyer waited for her here.

The injured worker sat beneath the remaining lean-to. His arm was bound close to his chest. Beside him stood the man who had helped cut pegs the day before, carrying a little box that rattled when he moved it.

"Put his name back," that man said.

The keeper looked up. "He cannot take his old station."

"He has a name whether he takes the station or not."

"I did not remove the name from the pay record."

"You're not showing us the pay record."

The keeper's face tightened. He had the appearance of a man whose shortage had been discovered in a part of him he thought properly private. He reached inside his coat for a narrow book, then seemed to reconsider. His hand remained there.

Jake remembered the freight coin in his own pocket. He had earned it for work done. It had not settled his pledge. He had carried it to the shelter because it was small enough to hold without anyone asking what else he meant to purchase with it. At the mill gate the coin seemed suddenly shameful. It had reached his hand while other people's names were being erased from a board.

"You could pay him from the timber," Jake said.

He had spoken before knowing which timber belonged to the mill and which to the workers who had rescued it, or whether any buyer would take what remained.

The keeper turned. "From what timber?"

Jake gestured toward the planks.

The daughter followed his hand. "That pile has not been tested."

"It looks sound."

"From here it does."

The keeper laughed once, without pleasure. "You would prefer we burn the rest of it to warm our hands?"

The injured man moved his good arm against his knee. "I would prefer you did not sell my wages as a promise and call the promise payment."

Jake took his hand down. He had confused a possible source of money with money actually owed, and for an instant he had offered both on behalf of people who had asked him for neither.

The keeper brought out the narrow book.

It was streaked with water along one edge. The pages opened reluctantly, as though the fire had made the ink into something thicker. The woman with red hands stepped forward to see. Jake stayed back. The names were small and close together; from his place he could make out only the columns.

"That is what was due before the fire," the keeper said. "Those sums do not vanish."

"Do they wait until the roof grows back?" she asked.

"I have no coin for them today."

The woman looked toward the injured worker. "Do you want me to speak for you?"

"I can speak," he answered. Then, with a gentleness that made the refusal more cutting, "Ask me again if the pain makes it difficult."

The woman nodded and fell quiet.

The worker asked the keeper to read his entry. The keeper read the days worked, the hours withheld pending an older dispute, and the amount he had actually received. He stopped before the last figure until the worker asked for it twice.

Jake felt the impulse to make the keeper say the figure a third time, loudly enough that everyone at the gate could hear the indignity. The worker had not asked for that. He was listening, his good hand flat on the bench as if he could press the account into something solid.

"Put down that I heard it," the worker said. "Not that I accepted it."

The keeper hesitated. Then he wrote.

The woman with red hands said, "And today?"

"No work at his old station."

"That isn't the question."

The younger man opened his box of wooden pegs. Each had a small flaw marked in charcoal; some could be cut shorter, others were warped beyond use. He carried the box to the bench and placed it where the worker could reach without twisting his injured arm.

"He can judge these by sight," the man said. "I'll do the cutting."

"How much of it is useful?" the keeper asked.

"We won't know until he has looked."

"Then I cannot name a day's wage."

The worker gave a short nod. "Name the work, then. And the hours. Do not put me in your empty column before you know what my hands can still do."

There was a pause while the gate wind moved chalk dust along the board. Jake had been expecting a speech from somebody, perhaps even himself. Instead the keeper turned the board over and drew a third column on the unmarked side. It was headed *Work to be measured*. Beneath it he wrote the injured man's name, followed by a blank space for hours.

This was not a payment. It was not even a promise of one. The worker looked at the new line, then at the box.

"I'll tell you when I begin," he said. "Not before."

The keeper left the book open on the ledge. The woman with red hands asked to see her own entry. A man behind her asked whether salvaging timber would count as mill work or hired work. Another asked who would test the wood once the damaged pieces were pulled away. The keeper had answers to some of these questions. For others he said he did not know.

The daughter had gone to the planks. She placed the rule on one, turned it, and put a cross in chalk on the end. The older wheelwright stood nearby, watching the angle of the grain.

"That one can be cut for a short brace," he said.

"After we inspect the other face."

"Yes."

"And not for the high loft."

He looked at the absent roof. "Not for the high loft."

The two of them took the plank out of the sale stack and laid it on the ground beside the small box. There was no occasion for congratulations. One piece of doubtful timber had found a possible use, and somebody would have to decide whether it was sound enough. The buyer who might have paid for a quantity of salvage would have one less piece offered to him. The keeper watched the plank leave his pile and made a note in the damp book. That much money, if money came, would not come from that length of wood.

The woman with red hands turned to Jake.

"Did you see the planks taken from the loft?"

"Only after the fire."

"Then you cannot say what they bore before it."

"No."

"Can you carry this empty box to the other bench?"

He picked it up. It weighed almost nothing. The bottom was split at one corner and his thumb fitted into the gap. He carried it to the place she indicated, set it square with the bench legs, and returned without asking whether she had any more work for him.

The injured man had begun looking over the pegs. He rejected the first three. He put the fourth to one side and asked for a shorter measure. The younger man fetched it. They could work for an hour and still discover there was no useful piece in the box. Their judgment was not the same as a wage, and a wage was not the same as the old debt. Jake was beginning to see how many different losses a person could carry without becoming one kind of victim.

At the gate, the keeper had hung the board with both sides visible. From the road the new column could be seen, but the figures in the book could not. The people who had come expecting the old workday could choose whether to stay for work that might be measured. Some remained. Two left, and no one called them disloyal.

Jake put the water vessel beside the washing stones at last. He had missed the morning freight queue; the man who hired carriers would have filled his places by now. The loss was his own. He was tempted to name it a sacrifice. Then he thought of the woman carrying the pay book inside to keep it from the rain, and he abandoned the grander word.

On the way back to the shelter he saw a cart stop at the outer road. The driver studied the lane toward the mill, then looked away toward the track beyond the houses. With the lower crossing still unsafe, strangers were finding reasons to ask where goods might travel. Jake did not go after him. He carried back the single fact he actually knew: some workers had stayed at the mill, their hours were not yet counted, and a board now admitted that there was work whose value had not been decided.

Mara was dividing the meal. The boy had laid out the uncracked bowls in a line and had found a use for the broken cup: it held a handful of clean pegs so they would not scatter underfoot. Jake told her about the board without saying the men had been paid.

"Will they be?" she asked.

"I don't know."

She put another portion into a bowl. "Then that is what you should say."

He sat down at the end of the table. The bowl in front of him had been chipped along the rim, but it held its contents. For a moment he watched the steam move toward the light. It was the kind of small fact people liked to use as a lesson: the vessel held, and so all the broken things might serve. He knew better now than to make the world confirm a sentence merely because the sentence sounded merciful.

Outside, the carts continued past the turning. Tomorrow he could follow the road far enough to find out where they went. He would not go on Mara's errand; she had given him none. The thought frightened him a little. After so many days explaining his choices as somebody else's summons, he was not sure how to describe a journey begun because he wanted to see for himself.
