## Part 16 — The Bed Under the Other Window: The Figure Beneath the Thumb

<!-- BEGIN 2016_CAUSAL THE_BED_UNDER_THE_OTHER_WINDOW_FIGURE_BENEATH_THUMB_CH19_V2_NONCANON.md | git_blob 7ba3820d444974617373645b1ec4f9973b1035ca -->

# The Bed Under the Other Window

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XIX V2 developmental candidate, preserving the original separately. NONCANON / independent editorial HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve eligible owner principals. Historical attribution of Jake to 2016 scoring slot 1 is not provider-authenticated. Mara, shelter occupants, labor, geography, dialogue, the courier's fate and any domestic arrangements below are invented secondary-world proposals, not approved Character Canon, Atlas or verified history. Follows Chapter XVIII's downstream disembarkation and inland journey. No 2016 Week 14 or postseason outcome enters character knowledge.*

### I. The windows before the door

The first light Jake recognized was not in the shelter.

It came from a house below the bend, where a woman stood at an open window shaking a cloth into the twilight. A little dust fell from it, white against the darkening hedge. She shook the cloth again and drew back. Somewhere beyond the house, two voices disagreed about the price of a basket. One began to laugh before the disagreement was over.

Jake had spent the last part of the road trying to compose an arrival. He would begin with his name, perhaps, though everyone in the shelter knew it. He would say that he had worked. He would put the receipt on the table so they could see that two days had belonged to something definite. Then he would offer the money toward his old pledge. In the version he preferred, Mara would not forgive him, exactly. She would look at the slip, see the work, and allow him to do what was necessary.

The woman's laughter below the bend was the first thing that interrupted this careful account. She had no knowledge of his journey, no reason to care whether the money had been earned or stolen from some foolish wager. Even the dust from her cloth would fall where it chose. He had mistaken the long walk for a kind of preparation that might oblige the people at its end to listen.

By the time he passed the last enclosed garden, he could distinguish the shelter's eastern roofline from the lower buildings. One corner of the roof was still covered by the darker temporary boards he remembered. The repaired threshold caught the last of the light. No one had raised the fallen part of the eastern wing. The canvas lodging beyond the wall lay low and wet-looking, its ropes stretched at an angle he could not judge from the road.

He slowed on the final rise.

He had expected to hear the clatter of provisions against the measure, the sharp complaint of the old counter, or Mara telling someone not to put a bucket where people were meant to walk. Instead he heard singing, if it could be called singing. It was one thin voice attempting the last line of a song and another voice interrupting to insist that the line belonged somewhere else. A third voice joined them and made the argument worse.

In the outer yard three children sat on the low stones where a cart had once been left waiting. One held a strip of red cloth over his head. Another had a wooden spoon and was beating it against his boot. The youngest saw Jake first.

"Are you bringing the rest of the song?"

"No," Jake said.

"Then you came too late."

The oldest child seized the cloth and continued singing. The youngest resumed his quarrel about the words. None of them asked where Jake had been. The spoon continued striking its boot with tremendous confidence.

He found this welcome so thin that it hurt.

At the shelter door the familiar latch lay on the shelf beside the jamb. A different piece of metal had been fitted temporarily in its place. He knew who had installed the last one because he recognized the uneven chisel mark. Someone had done it while he was gone. The replacement was not beautiful; it worked without complaint.

He put his hand on it and felt the cold through his split thumb.

Inside, the smell of the place had changed. There was still damp wool, smoke and boiled grain, but beneath them he caught the sharp scent of soap. Two small basins stood where the provision sacks used to be. On the wall a row of pegs had been added, each with a piece of cloth tied beneath it. The eastern quarter remained roped off. No one was sleeping beneath the uncertain beam.

A woman he did not know was reading from the account book at the long table. She held a stub of chalk between her fingers, not a pen. A broad-shouldered man carried jars past her and set them beside the hearth. At each jar she nodded or shook her head, and he changed the arrangement accordingly.

For a moment the man and woman might have been actors hired to stand in for people Jake remembered. Then the woman looked up and saw him.

"Are you seeking shelter?" she asked.

He had prepared an account of his journey, not an answer to that question.

"I used to sleep here."

"That wasn't what I asked."

He felt the old heat in his face. He had come to the right place; surely that entitled him to one sentence without a quarrel.

"I'm Jake."

The woman set down the chalk. "I know. Mara said you might return."

"Mara knows I'm coming?"

"She said you might. That's different."

The man with the jars took a breath as though he might speak, then carried the last vessel to the hearth. Jake watched it go. A place could change by an inch at a time until every familiar object was beyond his reach.

"Where is she?" he asked.

"Outside. Or at the wash place. I haven't counted her."

The woman glanced at the pack on Jake's shoulder.

"Put that against the wall until you know where you're staying. Please don't put it in the east room."

She resumed her book before he could ask who had given her the right to say it.

### II. The sleeper

The pallet had once been beneath the narrow western window, far enough from the hearth that its owner could sleep without listening to everyone who turned on the benches. It was not a private place. Jake had only made it private by returning to it so often that the other occupants began to leave it alone.

He crossed the room quietly, his hand already moving toward the corner where he kept his folded blanket.

Someone was lying there.

A young man, perhaps no older than Jake had been when he first came to the shelter, slept with one arm over his face. His boots stood together below the window. Beside them lay a square of wood with a badly carved bird in its center. The carving had been begun with ambition and continued with impatience. One wing spread wider than the other. There was a small dark spot on the board where the knife had cut too deep.

Jake stopped with the pack in his hand.

The sleeper was wearing the old gray blanket. Jake could tell from the seam he had repaired himself; one row of stitches angled sideways before returning to the edge. He had left the blanket behind, as a man leaves a cup on the table when he intends to return before supper. Now it covered someone else from shoulder to foot.

"That's my—"

The sleeper moved. His arm slipped from his face, revealing a bruise beneath one eye and the pale residue of plaster in his hair. Jake swallowed the rest of the sentence.

He could have said that he did not mind the person. It was the blanket that mattered, the place, the assumption that he would return. Yet when he imagined taking it back he saw the sleeper awakening cold and confused, looking for whoever had assigned him the pallet. He did mind the person, because the person had arrived without consulting him.

A pair of bare feet sounded behind him.

The smallest child from the yard stood at the doorway, red cloth still about his shoulders. He observed Jake staring at the bed.

"He's not dead," the child said.

"I didn't think he was."

"People who are dead don't snore like that."

The young man breathed through his mouth, very softly. The child considered him with the satisfaction of someone who had proved a fact.

"Does he live here?" Jake asked.

"Today."

"Since when?"

"Since the rain." The child held up the cloth. "It's a sail. Except sails have to be tied to a boat. We're trying to make one without a boat."

"Who gave him that blanket?"

The child looked at him as though he had asked who had given the sleeper his skin.

"It was here."

Jake felt ashamed of the question and angry that he had been made ashamed of it by someone who could scarcely keep the final line of a song in the proper place. The child began to demonstrate how the cloth would catch the air if he moved fast enough. It collapsed over his face. He pulled it away, disappointed but not defeated.

A hand touched the child's shoulder. It belonged to the broad-shouldered man with the jars.

"Outside, if you're going to fill the room with wind," he said.

The child went, carrying his red sail.

The man watched Jake watching the sleeper.

"His name is Len," he said. "The canvas at the far end didn't hold when the rain came. He was carrying a board out. It struck him. He'll move when he's fit enough."

"That was my bed."

"It was a place to sleep. It is a place to sleep."

"I know what it was."

The man looked at the pack, at Jake's hands, then at the sleeper. There was no challenge in his face, and that made the exchange worse. A challenge would have given Jake a clear opponent.

"We can find you boards for tonight," the man said. "But you'll have to ask Esi. She's arranging the dry spaces now."

"Esi?"

"The woman at the table."

"Mara arranges the beds."

"She hasn't been arranging them alone."

He lifted one shoulder and went to fetch water for the next jar.

Jake stood where he had stood before. On the far side of the room a woman opened a piece of bread and gave the softer half to someone whose face he could not see. The hearth stirred. Two people exchanged a basin. The sleeper remained asleep. His carved bird lay with its uneven wings pointed toward the door.

Jake turned away before Len woke.

His pack had grown heavy from being held rather than carried.

### III. What had not arrived

Mara came through the outer door carrying wet cloth in a low basket.

Jake knew the way she walked before he saw her face: with an uneven pace when she had been standing over the wash trough, one heel landing hard before the other followed. He had once thought the limp belonged to old age. He had never asked what had caused it. In his memory she was always already behind the table, ready to answer or to refuse him. He had difficulty imagining her anywhere else.

She set down the basket and recognized him without surprise.

"You're back."

"Yes."

She stood with her hands on the basket handles for a moment. The skin over her knuckles was swollen and pale from water. Jake had expected anger. He had prepared, even, to be equal to it. Her quietness made him uncertain where to begin.

"I worked upriver," he said. "Two days. We came down today."

"Are you hurt?"

"My thumb. Only a little."

"Did you wash it?"

"Yes."

"Keep it dry after you do."

She lifted the basket to the bench. The attention given to his hand was almost unbearable in its ordinariness. He had imagined a return large enough to make everything else stop. Mara was examining a torn sleeve, trying to see whether the seam could be repaired without turning the cloth inside out.

"I sent you a message."

Her hands stopped.

"Did you?"

"From the lower landing. The day after I stayed there. A man with a satchel said he'd take it to the door, if someone received it."

"What did you write?"

"That I had taken work. That you shouldn't count me for the water. That I still owed what I owed."

Mara set the sleeve aside.

"I haven't seen it."

"He may have come when you were away."

"Perhaps."

"He said the road might not hold."

"Perhaps it didn't."

Jake wanted the facts to become a defense: the paid courier, the right road, the difficulty of crossing, the care he had taken with the address. He had made one reasonable attempt. Surely that counted for something.

"I paid him."

Mara looked up, not sharply, not kindly.

"Did you buy a delivery or did you buy a promise to try?"

"A promise to try."

"Then you know what you paid for."

He did. The knowledge had been with him on the boat, at the landing, and all the way inland. It had been less oppressive when the money remained in his pouch and the miles were still ahead of him.

"Did you look for me?"

She pulled a thread from the sleeve. The question seemed to have surprised her less than it surprised Jake to hear himself ask it.

"We asked the people who came by the river road whether they'd seen a carrier with your description. One had seen someone who might have been you. Two had seen nobody. I didn't send people into the dark to search for a man who may have chosen to go farther."

"Why not?"

At once he wished the question unsaid.

Mara did not answer immediately. He could hear Esi moving the jars in the next room, the deliberate scrape of clay against the hearthstone.

"Because the east rooms are closed," Mara said at last, "and the coverings outside were taking water. Because we had a man struck in the face by a board. Because people here need dry places before they need me to guess where you've gone. What answer did you want?"

He had no answer he could bear to give. He wanted to say that a search would have proved his absence mattered. The sentence had a childish sound even in his own head.

"I thought you'd be angry," he said.

"I was."

"You're not now?"

"You came home with your boots on. Anger doesn't need to do all the work after that."

She rubbed at a stain in the cloth as if it might yield to one more pass of her thumb.

Jake laid Deren's folded receipt on the bench.

"Here. Two days."

She did not pick it up.

"I believe you worked."

"Read it."

"Why?"

He had crossed the river twice, followed hammer signals through an enclosed passage, and made himself leave a place offering regular pay. The words seemed too large to explain a square of paper.

"I want you to see I wasn't wandering."

"You could have wandered and returned. You could have worked and stayed away. I'm not asking which one makes the better story."

"It isn't a story."

"No. It happened. The question is what happens next."

He took back the receipt so hard that its corner tore. His thumb caught the paper. A sting of pain ran into his wrist.

"I came back," he said, louder than he intended. "I could have stayed. The hill offered me a room and a wage for a whole season. I left it. I got on the boat. I'm here."

Esi stopped moving the jars. The pause sounded through the doorway.

Mara's face changed then. The lines about her mouth drew tight.

"Don't tell me the shelter owes you gratitude for refusing a better bed."

"I didn't say that."

"You've told me the offer three times."

He looked at the torn corner of the receipt. He had wanted her to understand the scale of what he had refused. He had not known that telling her would diminish the refusal.

"I wanted to know whether I could come home."

"You can come through the door. You are standing in the room."

"That's not what I meant."

"Then say what you meant."

Jake looked toward the western window, where Len lay beneath the gray blanket. He wanted Mara to make him a place in the room; he wanted her to say the people here had suffered his absence; he wanted the wound he had left to fit exactly the shape of the sacrifice he believed he had made.

"I wanted you to have missed me," he said.

The words were so bare that he looked away.

For a long time Mara said nothing. A draft lifted a loose thread from the wet sleeve and laid it back again.

"I did," she said finally. "That was part of the trouble."

He looked up.

"And I needed to stop needing to know where you were before the water could be carried." She pushed the basket farther onto the bench. "Esi took the book when I couldn't hold it and the wash at the same time. She made a place for the injured lad. The others found hands. None of that was done to punish you."

Jake tried to make this sound like a comfort. It was not.

"If I stay," he asked, "can I have the book again?"

"It wasn't yours."

"I know."

"Do you?"

He thought of the days when he had carried the account to the hearth and felt useful because people moved aside for it. He thought of the newly tied cloth beneath the pegs, the jars that Esi had counted without consulting him, and Len asleep where he used to sleep. They had made a life that was neither the one he had left nor the one he imagined waiting to receive him.

Mara resumed mending the sleeve. She had not forgiven him. She had also not closed the door.

Jake could not decide which fact was harder to carry.

### IV. The figure beneath the thumb

A man came in carrying a shutter.

He carried it edge-first through the doorway, as though the room were a narrow passage on a boat and the wood might strike a rail. The pale grain showed the place where an old hinge had been pulled. Behind him came the shelter's former counter, holding one hand beneath the board without quite taking its weight. A square of chalk had blurred across its outer face, leaving a mark shaped like an open mouth.

"Where shall we put it?" the carrier asked.

Esi looked up from the account. "What is it for?"

"The east passage," said the counter. "We marked it for repair."

"The east passage is closed."

"Which is why it needs the shutter."

Esi came around the table. She put two fingers beneath the lower edge, testing the grain, and moved them away. The wood had split at the hole made for the hinge.

"It would close a window," Esi said. "It would not carry a roof."

"Nobody said it would carry a roof."

"You said it was for the passage."

"The same work, near enough."

"It isn't the same work."

The carrier held the board between them. Sweat had begun to darken his collar. Jake stepped forward and took one corner. The man yielded part of the weight without looking at Jake. Together they lowered it across two storage stools.

The counter unfolded his scrap. He named the amount set aside for the shutter, then the amount that remained due to the person who had cut it. Esi took the scrap. In the lower corner was a small hooked line Jake recognized. It had been used to mean a piece of work had been seen before money changed hands. Jake himself had drawn that shape when the tables were crowded and the counter wanted the queue to move.

Some marks had meant he had inspected the thing. Others, he remembered, had meant only that he stood near the person who requested it. The difference had seemed small then. It was large enough to fill the room now.

"That is your mark," the counter said.

Jake looked down. The hook was his. Near its end the lead had broken and been pressed into the paper again. He remembered the afternoon: the east quarter already roped off, the first report of the failing beam, someone asking whether the shutter could be made good. He had marked the request so the carpenter would not have to wait for somebody else to approve the measure.

"I made the mark," he said.

"Then the work is acknowledged."

"I never saw this shutter."

"You were away when it came."

"Then how can the mark acknowledge it?"

The counter glanced toward the doorway, where evening light made a thin bar across the stones.

"We used the same sign to carry the work forward. Nobody was hiding the board."

Jake wanted to insist that his intended meaning must be the true one. He had thought of a hook as a promise attached to the object it described, not something which could be unfastened and carried to a different piece of wood. But the paper was here, and the old counter had used it exactly as he claimed. The mistake had become useful to somebody while Jake was away.

"That sign was for an order," Jake said, "not a delivery."

"That's not how we used to read it."

"Then that's part of the trouble."

At the hearth a pot lid rattled. A girl with two bandaged fingers had been setting bowls on a low shelf. She stood holding one in her hand. The broad-shouldered man who carried the jars watched the board on its stools. Mara continued drawing a torn sleeve between her hands, testing where the seam would hold. Jake waited for her to correct him or the counter, as if the answer to the question he had just admitted might be found in her hands. She did not look up.

"Did the carpenter bring a bill?" Esi asked.

"Not to me," said the carrier. "I was paid to bring the wood. Someone said the house would show me where."

"Did anyone ask you to collect the rest?"

"No."

"Then you brought a shutter and a request. Those are two things."

The girl with the bowls shifted her weight. She could not pass the shutter easily to reach the hearth. Esi asked the carrier whether he had been promised something for the journey. A hot meal, he said, if the kitchen had one to spare. The man with the jars glanced at the pot.

"The kitchen can answer for the meal," Esi said. "I can answer for where the board goes."

She returned to the book and searched for an empty line. There was none among the crowded entries concerning the east rooms. A ragged seam of ink showed where water had crossed the page. Esi opened a fresh leaf, laid the folded scrap beside it, and wrote ORDER in letters broad enough to survive a wet edge. Beneath it she wrote RECEIPT NOT SHOWN.

She read both lines aloud.

"You will erase my mark?" Jake asked.

"No."

"Then he'll use it again."

"Not without the paper it was attached to."

She laid the scrap across the open book so both the counter and the carrier could see. The counter planted his fists on the table.

"You're undoing the way this house was kept."

"I'm keeping the difference between work requested and work seen."

Esi turned to the girl with the bowls. "Go through now. We can move it."

Jake took the shutter again. The carrier lifted the other end. They turned it carefully, past the shelf and out the threshold, with the damaged hinge toward the wall where it would catch no passing sleeve. Outside, the yard held the last warmth of the day. They set the shutter flat beneath the projecting shed. The carrier found two bits of broken tile and slipped them under its corners so the pale grain would not lie in mud.

"Not by the east door," Esi called from inside. "People will think it's open."

The carrier brushed the dust from his palms. He looked at the darkening road.

"Will you be going back?" Jake asked.

"By the same way, unless someone has blocked it."

"Can you tell the person who made it that payment hasn't been accepted?"

The man gave him a puzzled look. "I can tell him there's a board here. I don't know what the other part means."

Jake nearly explained the order, the hook, the book, the old clerk and the new. None of that would shorten the man's walk. The carrier had carried the shutter as he was asked, and all the shelter had given him was a place to leave it.

"Tell him Esi has kept the paper," Jake said. "He can speak to her when he comes."

"I'll tell him that."

The man crossed the yard toward the kitchen. Jake watched his shoulders until he went inside. No account would say how long he had held the board. No mark would tell the carpenter whether the journey had been worthwhile. The thing had arrived; the argument about what arrival meant was still here.

By the hedge the oldest child had found the red cloth again. He stretched it on two sticks set far apart, as the wheelwright's daughter had instructed. It caught the evening wind, bowed forward, and then dropped when the nearer stick slid through the child's fingers. The younger children cheered before it fell. They had been arguing so long over the sail that the argument had become its own kind of construction.

Jake brushed the grit from the shutter's edge. He wanted to lift it, carry it back to the workshop and demand an answer from the carpenter. He knew how such a gesture might look in a story: a man who had made an error bringing the mistaken object to its source. He also knew that he had not been asked to return anything. He did not know who would receive it or whether the carpenter was expecting payment for work already done. Sending the board away alone would be another answer made on someone else's behalf.

Esi was reading the count of bowls when Jake went back inside. The girl with the bandaged fingers corrected her once. Esi struck a line through the number, read the smaller total, and continued. Mara brought the finished sleeve near the hearth so its seam could dry. She did not ask about the shutter.

The counter remained by the book. He was looking at Jake as though the old hook might yet become what he wanted it to mean.

"I put my name to the request," Jake said.

"And that's all?"

"It's what I can answer for."

The old man looked as though he might curse. Instead he asked Esi where the carpenter should come. She said the covered entry, not the east passage, and she did not promise payment. The counter went out slowly, avoiding the girl carrying bowls. People came in behind him for supper, filling the space before Jake could decide what he had lost by speaking.

The two new lines were still wet. They had not repaired a roof or paid a bill or found a dry place for one more person. But when the carpenter came, he would have to begin with a thing Esi had actually seen, not with the shape of a mark made on an earlier day.

The smell of supper reached the yard. A child shouted that the sail was working again, followed almost at once by the clatter of sticks on stone. Nobody in the room went to look. Jake stepped outside instead.

### V. No seat kept warm

He went back into the yard because the shelter had begun to fill for the evening meal and he did not know where to stand.

The children had abandoned their song. One of them had found a length of string and tied the red cloth between two sticks. It moved a little in the wind, not enough to pull them over, but enough to make the smallest child shout that his sail had succeeded. Another child insisted that a sail which did not go anywhere was only a flag. They began to argue about the difference.

Near the wall a woman was repairing a wooden box. She held its side between her knees and used a short knife to scrape away a splinter that had caught in the hinge. Every few cuts she tested the lid. The little box had once contained nails. Jake recognized it from the mill yard. He had carried it there before the fire.

The woman was the wheelwright's daughter.

He had not expected to see her. The last time he stood before her he had wanted to make himself useful in the ruins of a workplace he had helped damage. Now she had brought part of its ordinary business beyond the mill. Beside her sat two finished wooden handles wrapped in cloth. They were not part of the great wheel. Their smooth grain and careful pegs made that plain.

"Are those for the shelter?" he asked.

She glanced up. "No."

"What are they for?"

"A buyer."

"I thought the mill was shut."

"It is."

"Where did you make them?"

"On a bench."

Her answers were not cruel. They were simply not offered as openings for his account of the upriver work. He saw the old signed measuring marks on the box and imagined telling her about the kiln passage and its signals, how the workers there would not let a sound become an order unless the nearest hand could confirm it. She, of all people, would understand the value of a craft whose authority came from knowing how something bore weight.

"I was at the stoneworks," he said.

"I heard someone say."

"The river took us there with four iron frames."

She continued scraping the box. "Did you help?"

"Yes."

"Did they pay you?"

"Yes."

"Good."

He watched her lift the lid, test the hinge, and shave away one more thin curl of wood. It fell to the ground like a strip of pale paper.

"There's work there for a season," he said.

"Then perhaps you'll go."

"I came back."

"You've said."

The words carried neither approval nor resentment. She put the knife away and set the repaired box beside the handles.

"Is your father's commission—"

"No," she said, before he finished. "And please don't ask him about it as though I sent you."

He had not meant to. He was suddenly certain that he had.

"Can I say something about the fire?"

"You can speak. Whether I have time to answer is another matter."

"I opened the service door because I thought the man inside couldn't get out. I didn't know what it would do."

"I know what you thought. I was there."

"That doesn't make it right."

"No."

He waited for the next word. There was none.

He could see her hands clearly now, the darkened skin at the edges of her nails, the fine red line across the back of one finger. She had worked through the day and come here with two finished pieces someone else had ordered. His confession did not make the handles smoother. His absence had not relieved her of making them.

"I am sorry," he said.

She lifted the finished work, tested its balance, and tied the cloth more firmly around it.

"I heard you."

He wanted her to say whether the apology had altered anything. Instead she stood, tucked the nail box beneath one arm, and began to carry her work toward the lane. The oldest child called to her to ask whether she could make his sail stand upright.

"Not tonight," she answered. "I'm carrying work that needs to be delivered."

The child looked disappointed. She stopped, considered the two sticks, and pointed to the ground between them.

"Put the lower ends wider. If you insist on holding them together, neither will stay."

He and the other child moved the sticks. The red cloth caught a slight breeze and held its shape for a moment. By the time they cheered, the daughter had gone.

Jake stood near the wall with the words of his apology still in his mouth. He had offered them because they were due. He had also wanted something in exchange. That want did not make the apology false, but it made him less certain what to do with the fact that she had heard it and continued on her way.

The child's sail fell. He laughed as though the fall were part of the game.

Jake watched him raise it again.

### VI. A night not promised

Esi found him after the last bowls had been carried inside.

She was shorter than Mara and spoke with the measured patience of someone who did not intend to repeat herself. In the fading light Jake could see chalk on the side of her hand.

"There's space under the western eave," she said. "It stays dry if the rain comes straight down. If the wind turns, you'll have to move your bedding."

"Len has the old pallet?"

"Len is using the old pallet."

"Is he going to recover?"

"His eye is swollen. The person who looked at it says he should rest. That's all I know."

Jake looked at her. "I wasn't asking for it back."

She looked toward his pack and then away.

"I didn't say you were."

The answer might have been merciful or merely exact. She pointed to a stack of loose boards beneath the eave, where the ground rose enough to keep a blanket from sitting directly in the wet.

"You can use two. Don't take the third; it holds the edge of the cover."

He followed her into the shelter to retrieve an extra cloth. Len had woken and was sitting upright on the pallet. His bruised eye had narrowed into a dark crescent. He held the carved bird between his fingers, worrying at the crooked wing.

"I was told you used to sleep here," Len said.

"Yes."

"Sorry."

The apology struck Jake harder than the displacement.

"You've nothing to be sorry for."

Len turned the bird so the wider wing faced the window.

"The cloth was already on it. I didn't know whose."

"Keep it tonight."

"Where are you sleeping?"

"Outside. Under the eave."

Len looked toward the open doorway, through which the smell of damp earth was entering.

"The rain came through the canvas," he said. "I thought the whole world had turned into a bucket."

Jake laughed before he could stop himself. It was a small sound, but it loosened something that had remained clenched since the landing.

"Did you carve that?" he asked.

Len held up the bird. "The left wing is wrong."

"Which is the left?"

Len pointed. Then he turned the wood over and pointed again, not altogether confidently.

"Maybe both. It was meant to fly."

"It looks as though it could."

"It hasn't."

Jake considered offering to improve the wing. The knife marks were deep and irregular; he could have made them more even. But he knew nothing about carving. He knew even less about the reason Len had made this particular bird. He let the object remain where its maker held it.

"I'll see you in the morning," he said.

Len turned it toward the window again. "If you don't go away."

The words were not accusation. They sounded like a practical uncertainty. Jake could not answer them with the receipt or the coins in his pouch. He had, only hours earlier, imagined an entire season elsewhere.

"I expect to be here," he said. "But you shouldn't count on an expectation."

Len gave him a puzzled look.

"All right," he said, as people do when someone has explained a small matter at greater length than it deserved. He laid the bird by his pillow and pulled the old gray blanket over his legs.

Outside, Esi had already put the boards aside. Jake placed them where she had indicated, not under the failing eastern quarter but along the western wall. He fetched water to wash his thumb, changed the bandage, and folded his pack beneath his head.

The yard was not empty. There were people moving about their own tasks, the last of the meal being put away, a child called indoors twice before he obeyed. Mara passed near the eave with her wet basket. She saw Jake, glanced at the boards, and stopped.

"Did Esi show you where to lie?"

"Yes."

"Good."

Jake sat up.

"I have the money," he said.

"I know."

"I haven't given you any of it."

"Then it is still in your pouch."

"I wanted to pay before anything else."

"Tomorrow we'll look at what you owe and what you can actually contribute. Tonight I'm tired."

He nodded. It was the first thing she had said that made him feel how much of the evening he had taken from her. She was holding the basket between them, its damp weight drawing her shoulders forward. His return had become another task she had to complete after work.

"Mara."

She waited.

"I know I can't buy back the days."

"No."

"I don't know whether I'm going to stay."

"Then don't promise that you are."

"I don't want to leave this unsettled."

"Then it may be unsettled for a while."

She moved toward the door.

He called after her, though he had no right to demand another answer.

"Will you still be here in the morning?"

Mara stopped in the doorway and looked back. There was a little light beyond her from the hearth, enough to make the outline of her face clearer than the expression it wore.

"I plan to be," she said. "Ask me again when it's morning."

She went inside.

Jake lay on the boards listening to the house settle. A loose piece of wood knocked beneath the eave when the wind rose, then became quiet. From the western window he could hear Len moving on the pallet and setting the carved bird down with care. Somewhere farther inside, Mara and Esi exchanged a few words too low for him to understand. They did not come out to ask where he wanted the water carried.

On the river, Deren had counted his wage in two groups. Here the coins remained together. They were neither enough to clear the pledge nor worthless because they could not. Tomorrow he could offer them without asking the room to celebrate what he had refused upriver. Tomorrow he could learn whether Mara would still be there to receive them.

For tonight he was in the western shelter of the wall, not in his old bed. Len was warm beneath the gray blanket. Esi had the book. Mara was going to sleep. The daughter had carried her finished handles away, and somewhere in the yard a sail made of red cloth had collapsed again.

Nothing had been restored to its former place.

He closed his eyes. The shelter did not become silent. It went on living.

- **Causal job:** Jake's return fails to restore the social position he left. A real replacement arrangement (Esi overseeing the book, Len occupying Jake's former pallet) and Mara's explicit admission she missed him force Jake to acknowledge that he both craved and resented being needed. He confesses the seasonal job offer and makes a voluntary apology to the daughter without receiving absolution. Overnight shelter is offered under the western eave only. No vow to remain, secured employment, repayment or reconciliation occurs.
- **Message:** Mara explicitly states she has not seen Jake's paid note. This does not prove the courier failed to travel or that no one accepted a delivery elsewhere; the courier's actual disposition remains UNKNOWN and can be investigated only through independent evidence in later draft fiction.

## Part 17 — The Cloth That Would Not Stay Folded

# The Cloth That Would Not Stay Folded

*2016 Commonwealth causal novel — Chapter XX candidate. NONCANON / independent editorial HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER. The episode follows Chapter XIX's return to the shelter and one night under the western eave. Mara, Esi, Len, Senn, their family circumstances, the weaving craft, roads, employment, dialogue, property and institutional arrangements below are invented secondary-world proposals rather than verified ESPN history or approved Character Canon/Atlas. Human Jake's binding to historical 2016 scoring slot 1 remains not provider-authenticated. No future season outcome is known to the fictional viewpoint.*

### I. A woman who did not ask for a bed

In the morning Jake found a narrow red thread beneath his boot.

He had spent the night beneath the western eave, waking whenever a loose board struck the wall. Before dawn the wind had turned. The roof still kept the rain from him, but a colder air had found the place between his shoulder and his blanket. His thumb ached where the bandage had loosened. He sat on the two boards Esi had allowed him, drew his coat about his knees and tried to remember why red thread should have been caught in the mud.

The children had made a sail of cloth the night before. He looked toward the stones where they had played. The sticks were gone, carried away or hidden for another game. The thread had come from elsewhere.

He bent to pick it up. It was not the stiff red of the child's sail but a finer shade, almost brown where the damp earth held it. One end was knotted around a fragment of pale wood so small it might have been a splinter. The knot had been made twice, each loop drawn tight in the same direction.

From the shelter came the sound of bowls being stacked. The room had been awake long before he had. Jake folded the thread into his palm, stood, and went to the washing place. He wanted to meet the morning without asking for his old pallet or the place behind the account book. He had rehearsed that modest ambition as he had once rehearsed his return. It felt suspiciously like another speech.

Esi was outside with a broom. She had tied a strip of cloth about her hair and stood with one foot on a flat stone, sweeping last night's damp ash out of the covered entry. Jake had not heard her approach. She looked at his bare hand.

"The bandage."

"It came loose."

"There's clean cloth inside. Take a narrow piece. The wider ones are for the pots."

He started to ask whether Mara had risen. Esi swept a line of ash toward the garden and said, before he could speak, "She's at the lower gate. Someone came for her."

"From the mill?"

"No. Someone who knows her."

The difference in Esi's tone made Jake follow the path around the wall.

A narrow wagon stood in the lane, its shafts resting on a pair of wooden blocks. It was not the mill cart, which remained elsewhere with its own troubles. This wagon was lower and narrower, with three bundles tied beneath a canvas cover and a shallow wooden chest strapped on the driver's bench. Its wheels had dry mud in the iron rims. A woman in a travel cloak stood beside it, examining a torn strip of cloth that had caught on one of the pegs.

She was not old. Her hair was graying at the temples, but she moved with the easy care of someone accustomed to lifting goods without help. She held the strip of cloth between her thumb and first finger, considering its weave. Beside her stood a driver with an empty bucket. He had set it on the ground and was waiting to be shown a water source.

Mara stood facing the woman.

Jake had never seen Mara quite still before. Even in anger she usually moved a hand toward a cloth or turned to see where a person had left a vessel. Now both hands hung by her sides. The basket she had been carrying lay on its side at her feet. One of its folded towels had fallen into the mud, and neither woman bent for it.

"You might have sent word," Mara said.

"I did."

"To whom?"

"To this place."

"We've had no letter from you."

"Then somebody owes me the price of a letter."

Mara's face had the expression she wore when the counters brought an agreement she had not been asked to read. But there was something else in it. Jake could not name it without claiming to know the history between them.

The traveling woman noticed him. She looked at his pack beneath the eave and then at the wash trough.

"Does he work here?"

"I don't know yet," Mara said.

"That's a peculiar answer."

"It's the only one I have."

The woman turned back to Jake.

"Will you show my driver where to fill a bucket? He doesn't need drinking water for the horses. Just enough to take the road dust off the axle before we set off again."

Jake pointed to the public trough beyond the hedge. The driver nodded and lifted his bucket without speaking. The stranger turned the torn cloth over, exposing a narrow row of faded stitches along its edge.

"What happened to your old curtain?" she asked Mara.

"We don't hang it now."

"Did you throw it away?"

"No."

"Then you still keep everything."

"Not everything."

They stood in another silence. The road beyond them filled briefly with the sound of a passing handcart. Its owner called to the driver to give way, and the driver moved his bucket aside. Ordinary traffic passed between the two women like a stream running across a stone.

Jake stooped for Mara's fallen towel. He held it by the clean corner.

"Where should I put this?"

"On the wall," Mara said, without looking at him.

The woman regarded him for another moment.

"You're the one who went upriver and came back?"

"I did."

"Then you aren't the only person who knows how to arrive at a door too late."

Mara's mouth tightened. "Senn."

There was a warning in the single word, but also a name spoken with such ease that Jake understood the woman had belonged to Mara's life long before the shelter existed.

Senn folded the torn cloth and placed it in her pocket.

"Good morning to you, sister," she said.

Mara did not answer. Yet when Senn moved toward the wagon to fetch its wooden chest, Mara stepped forward to help her lift it. Their hands found opposite handles without instruction. Together they carried the chest into the yard.

Jake watched their steps. They matched almost exactly on the uneven stones.

### II. The thing beneath the linen

The chest was small enough to be carried by two people and heavy enough that neither woman pretended it was light. They brought it into the covered entry, where the east rooms were still barred and the safer western passage remained in use. Senn set her end down on a flat stone. Mara waited until the chest had settled before letting go.

The lid was held by two leather loops. One had been replaced with new hide; the other was worn pale where a knot had rubbed it. Senn untied both, lifted the lid, and removed a bundle of cloth.

It was dyed in long uneven bands. Dark blue crossed a field of faded green, then disappeared into the brown of undyed wool. Along one edge small white knots protruded at regular intervals. The cloth had been folded so tightly that the creases had become ridges.

Mara's hand rose to the edge of the bundle and stopped.

"You brought that?"

"You wouldn't answer anything else."

"I didn't know you had it."

"I didn't know you wanted it until I found the note."

"What note?"

"The one you wrote before the winter road closed. The one you sent to Mother and then denied sending."

Mara glanced toward Jake. For a moment he thought she would ask him to go. Instead she pulled a wooden stool beneath the covered entry and sat with her hands open upon her knees.

Senn lifted the cloth by its corners. The folds would not release. They rose like walls even when she shook the bundle. She shook it again, impatiently, and a little dust drifted between the bands of blue.

"Don't," Mara said.

"It isn't glass."

"The edges are brittle."

Senn lowered her hands. "Then show me."

Mara rose at once. She took the cloth as though it were a living thing that might startle, spread it across the chest and worked each crease loose with her fingertips. Beneath the last fold lay a short wooden shuttle, narrower at one end than the other, with a blackened slot for the thread. The wood carried a shallow crack from tip to center. At one place it had been repaired with a brass pin too large for the hole.

Senn held it out.

"She kept that one. Not the new set."

Mara took the shuttle, but she did not inspect the crack. She turned it in her hands until the slot faced away from Jake.

"You don't have a loom here," Senn said.

"No."

"You used to be able to set a warp straighter than anyone in our house."

"That was a long time ago."

"Wood doesn't care how long ago you learned."

Mara put the shuttle on the chest. "What do you want?"

Senn drew a slow breath. "There's work in the lower valley. A broad loom has been standing idle since the beam broke. I have the frame. I have people willing to spin and dye, and a buyer who won't wait through another cold road. What I don't have is anyone who can put the threads back on it without wasting half the wool."

"Ask a weaver."

"I did. Three of them. They said the old beam wouldn't take it."

"Then they're likely right."

"They didn't look at the replacement."

Mara's eyes moved toward the wagon. Jake realized the long wrapped shape beneath its cover might not have been firewood after all.

Senn continued, speaking more quickly now. "I don't need you to promise a season. Come and look. Tell me where it's wrong. Stay three days if it can be repaired. Take your own wage, not mine. If you hate the place, I'll bring you back."

"You make it sound as though we're still girls."

"I remember how to ask you because the last time I tried asking any other way you refused."

Mara stepped back from the chest.

"I have people depending on me."

Senn looked toward the shelter door, where Esi was coming out with the account book. She had taken it to the dry passage to keep the pages from damp. The band of cloth around her hair had shifted in the work, and chalk marked the first finger of her left hand.

"Are these people unable to stand when you sit?" Senn asked.

Esi stopped a few paces from the chest. She looked from the folded cloth to the shuttle and then to Mara.

"I'm not answering for everyone," she said. "But I know how to open the stores."

Senn's face brightened. "There, you see?"

"That isn't what I said," Esi replied.

Jake moved away from the doorway to make room. He had come in to ask whether his water-carrying work might begin again. Now he stood beside a stranger's chest while two women spoke of a life Mara had never described. His own wages, his two days upriver, even the broken corner of Deren's receipt seemed to have been reduced to pocket things.

There were details he suddenly recognized. Mara had always tested cloth at the seam before she used it to bind a heavy jar. She could tell when a piece of linen would fray by drawing a finger over its edge. She once repaired a torn grain sack in a way that left the new row of stitches almost invisible. Jake had thought these were the ordinary skills of a woman who kept a shelter. He had never asked where she had learned them or whether she had wished to spend her life doing something else.

Senn spread the old cloth wider. A pale figure emerged near the center: not an animal, not a crest, but three uneven vertical lines bridged by a fourth, like a doorway drawn by someone who had known only its shadow.

"I found this at the bottom of Mother's cedar box," Senn said. "She hadn't finished it."

Mara touched the pale figure. Her fingers stopped at the third line.

"No," she said. "She finished it. She left the last bar open on purpose."

"How do you know?"

"Because I watched her do it."

Senn looked at the design for a long time.

For the first time Jake understood that the bundle was not merely bait to bring Mara to a loom. It was something over which the sisters still disagreed, perhaps something they had disagreed over since before he was born. He could not see their quarrel entire. He could see only the same cloth under both hands, and how neither hand let go of it.

Senn lowered her voice. "I can't mend that beam by myself."

Mara looked at the shuttle.

"You never tried to learn."

"I did. From you."

"That is not the same."

"No," Senn said. "I know."

The words were hard and gentle at once. Jake had no way to tell which of them carried the greater weight.

He slipped the red thread from his palm into his pocket. It was no longer something he meant to ask about.

### III. The cost of an empty chair

By noon the chest had become an obstacle.

People stepped around it to reach the water jars. A child tried to climb onto the lid and was lifted down by Esi, who said it was not a seat. When the bread was brought in, someone laid a tray on its flat top. Senn moved the tray without complaint and set the folded cloth beneath the lid, but the shuttle remained in plain sight.

Jake helped carry bowls to the western table. Len was awake at his window, sitting upright beneath the gray blanket with a bowl balanced on his knees. The bruise under his eye had changed color overnight. He held his wooden bird in his free hand, turning it now and then so that it caught the light.

"Who's the woman?" Len asked.

"Mara's sister."

"Does Mara have a sister?"

"Apparently."

Len considered that. "I had a brother."

Jake waited. Len scraped his spoon along the edge of the bowl.

"He used to say the wing on a bird is a kind of door. You open it against the air."

"Did he teach you to carve?"

"No. He made better ones." Len held up the crooked bird. "This is what I can do."

"Where is he now?"

Len looked at his bowl and ate another mouthful. Jake understood no more than that the question had been heard. He left Len with his meal.

When he returned, Esi had taken the account book to a narrow shelf away from the visitors. She was spreading out the day's small receipts, each pressed beneath a stone. She lifted her head when Jake approached.

"If you've come for a bed, I don't have a better one."

"I've come to settle what I can."

"Then sit. I need both hands."

He took the stool opposite her. It rocked on the uneven floor. Esi shifted a folded scrap of cloth under one leg, and it steadied. She opened a page marked with two narrow lines where an older entry had been crossed through and revised. At the top was Jake's old obligation. It stood among many others, neither honored nor condemned by its position.

"Some of this was already paid before you left," Esi said. "Mara entered the work. I did not strike it out."

"She told you?"

"She showed me the mark she made when the counter agreed. I don't need the story told again."

Jake took out his coins. The wages from the river had seemed substantial when Deren counted them on the boat. In the shelter, beside the amounts owed to the seller and the workers whose labor had not been finally measured, they looked very few.

"How much can I put against it?"

"As much as you're willing to part with. There's food and bedding to consider, and you have no guaranteed work tomorrow."

He put two coins on the table. He thought of the promised seasonal wage upriver, and felt the warmth of embarrassment that came whenever he remembered how easily he had explained the offer to Mara. Esi moved neither coin.

"Keep one," she said. "You may need to travel. I won't record a payment you have to borrow back before dark."

"I could give both."

"Yes. You could."

Her voice left the decision entirely in his hands. It was more difficult than being ordered. He took back one coin.

Esi recorded the other in the open line, read the amount aloud, and gave him a short receipt. She turned the book so he could see the updated entry. The remainder was still there. No part of the original supplier's claim had been forgiven, no grain made safe by the movement of ink, no wage conjured out of a promise. The number was smaller because one coin was no longer his.

"That's the part you paid," she said. "Not the part you meant to pay."

He folded the receipt without answering.

From the doorway came Senn's voice.

"Did she always take so long to make up her mind?"

Mara was near the hearth, cutting linen into strips. Her scissors stopped.

"Sometimes I made up my mind while you were still asking the question."

"You decided to go before I could finish asking why."

The cloth fell from Mara's hands. She looked across the room at her sister.

Jake had not heard the beginning of the argument. Senn stood in the doorway with the old shuttle in one hand and the travel cloak in the other. He could see that she had not intended to speak so loudly; she looked at the listening occupants, then at Mara.

"This isn't the place," Senn said.

"You chose the place."

"I chose the only door I was told you still opened."

Mara walked toward her. They went into the yard. Jake remained beside Esi, who was blotting the ledger with a clean scrap. He had every reason to turn back to his own affairs. But the shelter door stood open, and the women's voices carried through it.

"You left after the burial without speaking to me," Senn said.

"I spoke. You were standing beside the wagon."

"You said the road was passable."

"It was."

"You didn't say you were leaving for good."

Mara answered too quietly for Jake to hear.

"Do you remember the cloth?" Senn asked. "Do you remember what she told us to do with the end?"

A pause.

"To leave enough thread for the next hand," Mara said.

"And you took your hand away."

Jake could not see them from where he sat. The words arrived without the faces that might have changed their meaning. He felt the temptation to fit the two sisters into a judgment: one had abandoned the other, one had remained, one had been asked to pay for the other's absence. He had spent the previous evening in the same argument with Mara, but its likeness did not grant him knowledge of this one. He stayed at the table.

Esi closed the book.

"You've been staring at the same part of that receipt."

"I wasn't trying to listen."

"The door is open."

"I should shut it."

"If you close it now they'll know you've been listening."

She returned the book to its shelf.

"Does she have to go?" Jake asked.

"Who?"

"Mara."

Esi looked at him for so long that he wished he had framed the question differently.

"That's for her to decide."

"And the shelter?"

"The shelter is here."

"We need someone who knows the supplies."

"I know the supplies."

"Someone who can mend—"

"I can mend what I know how to mend. If a thing needs more, I'll ask someone who knows more."

Esi untied the cloth from her hair and retied it more tightly. She was not triumphant. She was tired. Jake saw the stain on the side of her thumb where the chalk had worked into a split in the skin.

"Three days?" he asked.

"If she goes, perhaps three days. Perhaps longer. A road has not signed an agreement to return her. I'll tell people what I know when I know it."

A shout rose from outside. It was the child with the red sail calling for a missing stick. Esi went to the door, looked at the stones where he had been playing, and told him that nobody would help him search for a stick while the water jars remained empty. He ran off with a cry of indignation, already looking in the wrong place.

Jake looked at his receipt. The entry had changed that morning. He could see the ink. Nothing about the day had become orderly.

### IV. The shuttle and the wheelwright's daughter

The wheelwright's daughter arrived in the middle of the afternoon carrying a narrow bundle wrapped in clean sacking.

She did not come for Jake. She entered the covered yard, asked Esi whether the buyer's messenger had called, and set the bundle on a dry board when told he had not. The handles she had finished the previous evening remained wrapped together, their pegs cut to a precise fit. She had come to collect her own payment, not to settle anyone else's promise.

Senn noticed the bundle.

"Are those new handles?"

"Yes," the daughter answered.

"Who cut them?"

"I did."

Senn reached toward the sacking, then stopped. "May I see the joint?"

The daughter untied one corner and showed her. Senn examined the line where two woods met. She ran a finger close to the peg, not touching it.

"That's clean work. I have a beam with a split. Not here. A day and a half by the lower road, if the weather holds."

"How wide?"

Senn named a measure. The daughter asked where the split began, whether the wood had been wet, and whether the frame had twisted when the beam failed. Senn could answer the first question and only part of the second.

"Then I can't tell you it can be repaired," the daughter said.

"Can you come look?"

"Not today. These must go to their buyer, and I have another delivery tomorrow."

Senn glanced at Mara, who was standing near the open chest.

"What would it cost for a look after that?"

The daughter considered. "The journey each way, a day's examination, and a separate price if the repair is sound. Travel is paid whether I can save the beam or not."

"That's more than I expected."

"It is what it costs me to stop my other work."

Jake watched Senn's hand close around the shuttle. At the mill yard, when the daughter's independent commission had been jeopardized, he had asked questions as though an answer might allow the work to begin again by force of understanding. Here Senn was dealing with the daughter as a craftswoman whose absence from her own bench had a value. Their disagreement was not a moral ceremony. It was the price of distance, time, and an uncertain beam.

"I can't pay you that today," Senn said.

"Then I'll go about my work."

The daughter began to tie the sacking.

"Would you at least look at this?" Mara asked.

She handed over the old shuttle. The daughter turned it under the light. She examined the black slot, pressed a thumbnail against the brass pin, and laid the cracked tip against her palm.

"It's been repaired once with too much metal," she said.

"Can it still carry thread?" Senn asked.

"It can carry thread now. I wouldn't put it under much pull. The pin will split it again."

"Could you make another?"

"Yes. Not before the morning."

Senn looked toward the wagon. Its driver was tying the water bucket beneath the bench and testing the knot by pulling it sharply.

"We leave at first light."

"Then take care with the one you've got," the daughter said.

Mara reached for the shuttle. She turned it over once in her hands, measuring the length with her thumb. Her fingers appeared swollen from years of washing cloth and carrying baskets, but they remembered the tool's shape well enough to find each worn curve without searching.

"There's a smaller one," Mara said, almost to herself.

Senn looked up. "Where?"

"At the bottom of the second drawer. If you didn't throw it away."

"I never saw it."

"Mother kept her narrow shuttles under the short spools. She didn't count them as part of the loom."

"She didn't count half her life as part of anything."

Mara's fingers tightened on the old wood. The daughter's gaze moved between the sisters. She made no attempt to join their account of the dead woman.

"If you want me to inspect the cracked one later," the daughter said, "bring it to the bench. If the wood is sound enough, I can replace the pin and cut a sister piece for it. But don't promise a buyer that the old shuttle makes the old loom work. Those are different things."

She bound the handles, thanked Esi for keeping the sacking dry, and carried her bundle toward the lane. The children called for help with the sail. She told them she would be late for her buyer if she stopped. They returned to their sticks without protest.

Senn watched her leave.

"She's not easy to persuade."

"She's working," Mara said.

"I can see that."

"Can you?"

Senn began to answer, then stopped. She looked toward the folded cloth in the chest. The woman who had driven all this way with a proposal had found, in an afternoon, how much she did not know about her own materials.

"I can ask someone else," she said.

"You've already asked three."

"Then perhaps there is no one."

Mara lifted the shuttle. "There is someone who can look at the threads. That is all I have offered."

Senn's shoulders changed. They did not relax completely, but she set her travel cloak on the wagon instead of drawing it over her arms.

"Three days?"

"Three nights away, perhaps. I can't tell you when the road will open or what we'll find when we get there. If the frame is beyond use, I will say so."

"And if it isn't?"

"We'll decide what to do with it after we've seen it."

Senn laughed, once, with no pleasure in it.

"I remembered your hands," she said. "I forgot your conditions."

Mara held out the shuttle. Senn took it, then gave it back. Neither woman seemed to know who ought to carry it.

Jake could see the beginning of a departure. He could not see its end.

### V. A place for the absent

That evening Mara packed very little.

Jake saw her come out with a square bundle tied by two plain cords. It held a change of linen, a cup with its handle mended in wire, and a knife too blunt for any of the work he had seen her do. The old shuttle lay across the top. She set the bundle on a bench while Esi inspected the western roof.

Senn had gone to make certain the driver could keep the wagon in the lane overnight. She had asked permission to stable the animal elsewhere and had paid for the water it drank. Nothing about her visit had been free of effort or cost. Jake wished there were a single account that could hold that fact beside the argument between the sisters without pretending the two canceled one another.

Mara opened her hand to show Esi a set of keys.

"That one is for the upper cupboard," she said. "The bent one opens the chest with the clean cloth. Don't force it when it sticks. The narrow one belongs to the drawer. The east boundary stays closed."

"I know."

"If Len's eye worsens, send for someone who can examine it. Don't ask him to haul boards before he's fit."

"I know that too."

Mara looked at the keys, then closed Esi's hand around them. There was a small pause. It was the sort of pause Jake had seen after an agreement that changed more than either speaker wished to admit.

Esi said, "There is a place for your bundle if you return late."

"I haven't asked you to keep one."

"I'm telling you where it will be."

Mara almost smiled. Then she drew Esi close and held her for a moment. Esi stood stiffly at first, her hands at her sides. When Mara released her, Esi wiped one eye with the back of her sleeve and went to move a basin that had not been in anyone's way.

Jake remained by the bench.

"Am I meant to carry your bundle?" he asked.

"No. I can carry it."

"Then what do you want me to do?"

She looked toward the western wall, where his boards remained under the eave.

"Whatever you decide tomorrow, tell Esi before she counts you for a meal."

He almost objected. A person might leave to mend an old loom and still be allowed to ask someone to stay. He wanted Mara to make a request that would have the weight of their history behind it. She had asked him to repair the latch, carry water, write a correction, find someone missing. Now, on the one day he might have been ready to accept a command, she offered none.

"I put something against the debt," he said.

"Esi showed me."

"It's only part."

"I can see."

"Does it matter?"

"Yes."

She did not add anything, and for once Jake did not ask her to.

He watched her wrap the shuttle in the striped cloth. It would not lie flat. Each time she folded an edge over the cracked wood, another fold rose up where the cloth remembered a different shape. She tried twice and then stopped trying, laying the bundle open within the larger square of linen.

"Will you be safe?" he asked.

"Safer than someone who assumes the road has signed a promise."

He had heard that voice before, turned against imprudent suppliers and men who said a roof was sound because it had not yet fallen. But she was not speaking now as keeper of the shelter. She was a woman with an unfinished argument waiting on the lower road.

In the western room Len had begun whittling the bird again. His knife made a light, uneven tapping against the wood. Esi called to him not to scatter shavings into the wash basin. He answered that the bird had chosen a poor place to be born. She told him to choose a better one.

The talk continued after Mara had gone to sleep.

At first light the driver brought the wagon to the gate.

The animal shook its head against the straps. Senn checked the binding on the long covered beam and moved the chest farther back to balance the load. She had dark half-moons under her eyes. Mara came out of the shelter holding the striped bundle. The air was cold enough that her breath was visible when she spoke.

"Keep away from the deep rut beyond the orchard," Mara told the driver.

"I know that road," he said.

"Then you know its ruts."

Senn opened the wagon door and made room beside the chest. Mara looked back toward the shelter. Esi stood with the keys in one hand. Len had come to the western window and was holding up his bird, as though offering a passenger for the journey. The little wing caught the dawn light.

Mara raised two fingers toward him. He lowered the bird.

For a moment Jake expected her to turn, take the keys back, say there was too much work to be done, or invent some other necessity. She did none of those things. She climbed into the wagon and drew the cloth bundle onto her lap.

Senn took the seat beside her. Before the driver moved, she leaned toward the open side and called to Jake.

"You found a thread this morning."

He put his hand in his pocket. The thread was still there, curled around its little piece of wood.

"It caught on the wagon peg when we turned in," she said. "I meant to pick it up."

"Do you want it?"

Senn studied the small knot in his fingers.

"No. Keep it. There are more of those than I can use."

Mara said something Jake could not hear. Senn turned to answer. The driver clicked his tongue, and the wagon began to move.

Jake watched it pass the last enclosed garden. The chest shifted gently at every rut. Senn put a hand on it without interrupting her argument, whatever it had become. Mara leaned forward to look at the road. They did not look back together.

After the wagon had disappeared, Esi went inside. She began calling the morning amounts: how much clean water remained, which cloth had been dried, whether anyone had seen the missing ladle. The answering voices came from different parts of the shelter. Some were uncertain. Some were cross. Someone had already taken the ladle to the washing place. No one had to agree about Mara before the day could begin.

Jake stood at the gate with the red thread between his fingers. He did not tie it around his wrist or save it as a token of a promise. When the smallest child came running through the yard, calling that the sail needed a new line, Jake opened his hand.

"Will this do?"

The child took the thread and tested it between two fingers.

"Not by itself. It's too short."

"Then you may have to join it to another."

"Of course," the child said, as if he had proposed nothing worth asking.

He ran toward the stones, carrying the little fragment of wood with the knot still tight about it.

Jake turned toward the door. Len's carved bird was no longer in the window. Esi was inside, arguing with the cook over an amount of salt. Mara's work basket stood beneath the bench where she had left it, one handle worn pale from years of use.

Jake lifted the basket to keep it out of the way of the water carriers. He set it on the shelf by the doorway, not at the head of the table and not out of sight. The worn handle faced the room.

Esi called for the next jar. He went to fetch it.

---
